# More adjectives: Q–S

> Adjectives Q–S beyond the grammar groups — the everyday adjectives that a triage of WordNet’s adjective lemmas showed a learner needs.

[← The grammar of adjectives](../README.md) &middot; [All groups](README.md)

Most English adjectives behave: they go before a noun or after *be*, and they compare with *-er* or *more*. These are those — but each still says how it compares, where it can stand, and what it takes. WordNet lists about twenty thousand adjectives; most of the rest are technical, archaic or British-only, and a triage pass left them out on purpose.

---
### Qatari

**Pronunciation:** /kəˈtɑːri/ &middot; *kuh-TAH-ree*
**Русский:** катарский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Always capitalized. *A Qatari* for a person is a noun, not an adjective.

From Qatar, or relating to Qatar and its people.

1. A *Qatari* airline sponsors the tournament.
2. The stadium was built with *Qatari* money.
3. My lab partner is *Qatari*.

---

### quack

**Pronunciation:** /kwæk/ &middot; *KWAK*
**Русский:** шарлатанский, знахарский
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*a quack* is a fake doctor) and a verb (ducks *quack*). As an adjective it goes only before a noun: say *The remedy is a fraud*, not *The remedy is quack*.

Falsely claiming to have medical knowledge or to cure people.

1. He spent thousands on *quack* remedies.
2. A *quack* doctor sold them fake vitamins.
3. The clinic pushes *quack* cures for cancer.

---

### quadratic

**Pronunciation:** /kwɑːˈdrætɪk/ &middot; *kwah-DRAT-ik*
**Русский:** квадратный (об уравнении), квадратичный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable

In math: involving a quantity multiplied by itself, that is, squared.

1. We solved the *quadratic* equation in class.
2. She still remembers the *quadratic* formula from tenth grade.
3. The growth is *quadratic*, not linear.

---

### quadrilateral

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkwɑːdrɪˈlætərəl/ &middot; *kwah-dri-LAT-ur-uhl*
**Русский:** четырёхсторонний, четырёхугольный
**Type:** shape
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** *quadri-* (four) + *lateral* (sided)
**Contrast:** Also a noun: *a square is a quadrilateral*.

Having four straight sides.

1. A trapezoid is a *quadrilateral* shape.
2. Draw any *quadrilateral* figure on the board.
3. The old fort is roughly *quadrilateral*.

---

### quadruple

**Pronunciation:** /kwɑːˈdruːpəl/ &middot; *kwah-DROO-puhl*
**Русский:** четырёхкратный, учетверённый
**Type:** quantity
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** *quadru-* (four) — compare *double*, *triple*
**Contrast:** Also a verb (*Sales quadrupled last year*). For the surgery and the crime the noun after it stays singular: *a quadruple bypass*, *a quadruple homicide*.

Made up of four parts, or four times as big or as many.

1. He had a *quadruple* bypass last spring.
2. Police are investigating a *quadruple* homicide.
3. She landed a *quadruple* jump in competition.

---

### quaint

**Pronunciation:** /kweɪnt/ &middot; *KWAYNT*
**Русский:** причудливый, старомодно-милый
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est or more/most — *quainter*, *quaintest* or *more quaint*, *most quaint*
**Contrast:** It can sound condescending: calling someone's beliefs *quaint* says they are charmingly out of date.

Quaint has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Attractive in an old-fashioned way.** (старомодно-милый, уютный)

1. They stayed in a *quaint* little inn on the coast.
2. It's the *quaintest* town in New England.

**2. Odd or out of date in a way that amuses you.** (причудливый, забавно устаревший)

3. He has a *quaint* habit of writing thank-you letters.
4. The idea of a landline seems *quaint* now.

---

### qualitative

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɑːlɪˌteɪtɪv/ &middot; *KWAH-li-tay-tiv*
**Русский:** качественный (не количественный)
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *quantitative*
**Contrast:** A false friend. Russian *качественный* usually means *of good quality* — that is *quality* or *high-quality* in English. *Qualitative* only contrasts with *quantitative*: about kind, not about amount.

Relating to the nature or kind of something rather than to amounts.

1. The study used *qualitative* interviews instead of surveys.
2. There is a *qualitative* difference between the two recordings.
3. Her research is mostly *qualitative*.

---

### quality

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɑːləti/ &middot; *KWAH-luh-tee*
**Русский:** качественный, высокого качества
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Mostly a noun (*the quality of the work*). As an adjective it goes only before a noun; after a verb say *high-quality*: *The tools are high-quality*, not *The tools are quality*.

Of a high standard.

1. They sell *quality* furniture at a fair price.
2. We try to spend *quality* time together on weekends.
3. *Quality* childcare is hard to find here.

---

### quantifiable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɑːntɪˌfaɪəbəl/ &middot; *KWAHN-ti-fy-uh-buhl*
**Русский:** измеримый, поддающийся подсчёту
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quantifiable*, *most quantifiable*
**Form:** -able from *quantify*

Able to be measured or expressed in numbers.

1. The benefits are real but not easily *quantifiable*.
2. Give me *quantifiable* goals for the quarter.
3. Air quality is more *quantifiable* than customer mood.

---

### quantitative

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɑːntɪˌteɪtɪv/ &middot; *KWAHN-ti-tay-tiv*
**Русский:** количественный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *qualitative*

Relating to amounts and numbers.

1. The report includes *quantitative* data from 3,000 users.
2. He works on the *quantitative* side of the fund.
3. Their evidence is *quantitative* rather than anecdotal.

---

### quarantined

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɔːrənˌtiːnd/ &middot; *KWOR-uhn-teend*
**Русский:** находящийся на карантине, изолированный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *quarantine*

Kept away from other people or animals so that a disease cannot spread.

1. The *quarantined* passengers stayed on the ship for a week.
2. Our whole class was *quarantined* after the outbreak.
3. New birds are kept *quarantined* for thirty days.

---

### quarrelsome

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɔːrəlsəm/ &middot; *KWOR-uhl-suhm*
**Русский:** сварливый, склочный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quarrelsome*, *most quarrelsome*
**Form:** -some from *quarrel*

Arguing often, and looking for a fight.

1. Two *quarrelsome* neighbors dragged the whole street into court.
2. He gets *quarrelsome* after a couple of drinks.
3. The committee is more *quarrelsome* than it used to be.

---

### quarterly

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɔːrtərli/ &middot; *KWOR-tur-lee*
**Русский:** ежеквартальный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ly from *quarter* — an *-ly* adjective, not an adverb here
**Contrast:** *Quarterly* is also an adverb (*The board meets quarterly*) and a noun (a literary *quarterly*). As an adjective it stays before the noun: *the quarterly report*.

Happening or produced four times a year.

1. The *quarterly* report comes out in April.
2. Freelancers pay *quarterly* taxes.
3. She writes for a *quarterly* literary magazine.

---

### queasy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwiːzi/ &middot; *KWEE-zee*
**Русский:** испытывающий тошноту; неспокойный
**Type:** emotion; also quality
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *queasier*, *queasiest*
**Takes:** *about* + noun or *-ing*

Queasy has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Feeling as if you might vomit.** (испытывающий тошноту)

1. Boats make me *queasy*.
2. I felt *queasier* with every curve in the road.

**2. Uneasy and worried about something.** (неспокойный, встревоженный)

3. Investors are *queasy* about the merger.
4. The whole plan gives me a *queasy* feeling.

---

### queen-size

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwiːnˌsaɪz/ &middot; *KWEEN-syz*
**Русский:** размера «квин» (кровать 152×203 см)
**Type:** size; also compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *queen* + *size*; *queen-sized* is just as common
**Contrast:** A US bed-size word. From small to large: *twin*, *full*, *queen-size*, *king-size*. A *queen-size* bed is about 60 by 80 inches.

Of a bed or bedding: fairly large — bigger than a full, smaller than a king.

1. The room has a *queen-size* bed.
2. We need *queen-size* sheets, not king.
3. A *queen-size* mattress won't fit up those stairs.

---

### queer

**Pronunciation:** /kwɪr/ &middot; *KWEER*
**Русский:** квир, ЛГБТ; странный (устар.)
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — in the identity sense; the old *strange* sense took *queerer*, *queerest*
**Contrast:** Once a slur, now reclaimed and used neutrally by many LGBTQ people, in academic writing and in *queer studies*. Still, do not apply it to an individual who has not used it of themselves. The *strange* sense is old-fashioned and now sounds odd in American English.

Queer has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Relating to people who are not heterosexual or not cisgender.** (квир, ЛГБТ)

1. She writes about *queer* history in the Midwest.
2. The festival celebrates *queer* artists.
3. He came out as *queer* in college.

**2. Old-fashioned: strange or odd.** (странный, чудной)

4. There was something *queer* about the whole story.
5. He felt a bit *queer* after the shot.

---

### querulous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɛrələs/ &middot; *KWER-uh-luhs*
**Русский:** ворчливый, брюзгливый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more querulous*, *most querulous*
**Contrast:** Not related to *query*. It describes a whiny, complaining tone, not a questioning one.

Complaining in a weak, irritated way.

1. A *querulous* voice came from the back row.
2. He grew *querulous* in his last years.
3. Her emails get more *querulous* every week.

---

### questionable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɛstʃənəbəl/ &middot; *KWES-chuh-nuh-buhl*
**Русский:** сомнительный, небезупречный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more questionable*, *most questionable*
**Form:** -able from *question*

Probably not true, honest or good — worth doubting.

1. The numbers came from a *questionable* source.
2. Several of the expenses look *questionable*.
3. His taste in movies is even more *questionable* than mine.

---

### questioning

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɛstʃənɪŋ/ &middot; *KWES-chuh-ning*
**Русский:** вопросительный, недоумевающий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** more/most — *more questioning*, *most questioning*
**Form:** -ing from *question*

Showing that you have doubts or want an answer.

1. She gave me a *questioning* look.
2. He raised a *questioning* eyebrow.
3. Students today take a more *questioning* attitude toward authority.

---

### quick

**Pronunciation:** /kwɪk/ &middot; *KWIK*
**Русский:** быстрый; короткий; сообразительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *quicker*, *quickest*
**Takes:** *to*-infinitive — *quick to blame*
**Opposite:** *slow*
**Contrast:** The adverb is *quickly*. *Come quick!* is informal speech; in writing use *quickly*. *Fast* is about speed of movement, *quick* often about a short time: *a fast car*, *a quick trip*.

Quick has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Moving or happening fast.** (быстрый, скорый)

1. She's a *quick* walker — I can never keep up.
2. Is there a *quicker* way downtown?
3. He's always *quick* to blame someone else.

**2. Taking only a short time.** (короткий, быстрый (недолгий))

4. I took a *quick* shower and left.
5. Can we have a *quick* word before the meeting?

**3. Able to understand and learn fast.** (сообразительный)

6. She's *quick* — she picked up the software in a day.
7. He has a *quick* mind and a sharp tongue.

---

### quick-tempered

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkwɪkˈtɛmpərd/ &middot; *kwik-TEM-purd*
**Русский:** вспыльчивый
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quick-tempered*, *most quick-tempered*
**Form:** compound — adjective + noun + *-ed*
**Opposite:** *even-tempered*

Getting angry very easily.

1. His father was *quick-tempered* and everyone in town knew it.
2. She's less *quick-tempered* than she was at twenty.
3. A *quick-tempered* coach can lose a locker room fast.

---

### quick-witted

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkwɪkˈwɪtɪd/ &middot; *kwik-WIT-id*
**Русский:** находчивый, остроумный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quick-witted*, *most quick-witted*
**Form:** compound — adjective + noun + *-ed*
**Opposite:** *slow-witted*

Able to think and answer fast, often in a funny way.

1. The host is *quick-witted* and never loses the room.
2. A *quick-witted* clerk stopped the scam.
3. You have to be more *quick-witted* than that to do stand-up.

---

### quilted

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɪltɪd/ &middot; *KWIL-tid*
**Русский:** стёганый
**Type:** material; also participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *quilt*

Made of two layers of cloth sewn together with padding in between.

1. She wore a *quilted* jacket over her sweater.
2. The bedspread is *quilted* by hand.
3. *Quilted* oven mitts protect your hands better.

---

### quintessential

**Pronunciation:** /ˌkwɪntɪˈsɛnʃəl/ &middot; *kwin-ti-SEN-shuhl*
**Русский:** типичнейший, воплощающий суть
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable — it already means *the most typical*
**Form:** from *quintessence* — the purest form of something

Being the most perfect example of a type.

1. He's the *quintessential* New Yorker — loud, kind, always late.
2. Diners are the *quintessential* American restaurant.
3. That album is *quintessential* seventies rock.

---

### quirky

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɜːrki/ &middot; *KWUR-kee*
**Русский:** чудаковатый, с причудами
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *quirkier*, *quirkiest*
**Form:** -y from *quirk*

Unusual in a likable, slightly odd way.

1. It's a *quirky* little bookstore with a cat in the window.
2. Her second novel is even *quirkier* than the first.
3. He has a *quirky* sense of humor.

---

### quixotic

**Pronunciation:** /kwɪkˈsɑːtɪk/ &middot; *kwik-SAH-tik*
**Русский:** донкихотский, прекраснодушный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quixotic*, *most quixotic*
**Form:** from *Don Quixote*
**Contrast:** The pronunciation does not follow the name: kwik-SAH-tik, not kee-HOH-tik.

Having generous, idealistic plans that have almost no chance of working.

1. He launched a *quixotic* run for governor.
2. The plan is noble but more *quixotic* than practical.
3. Her *quixotic* campaign to save the old theater lasted two years.

---

### quizzical

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwɪzɪkəl/ &middot; *KWIZ-i-kuhl*
**Русский:** недоумевающий, вопросительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quizzical*, *most quizzical*

Showing that you are puzzled, often with some amusement.

1. He gave her a *quizzical* look.
2. She raised one eyebrow in a *quizzical* expression.
3. The second question got an even more *quizzical* look than the first.

---

### quotable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈkwoʊtəbəl/ &middot; *KWOH-tuh-buhl*
**Русский:** меткий, годный для цитирования
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more quotable*, *most quotable*
**Form:** -able from *quote*

Worth quoting, or saying things that people like to repeat.

1. The coach is endlessly *quotable*.
2. It's the most *quotable* movie of the decade.
3. He wrote a short, *quotable* line for the poster.

---

### rabble-rousing

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræbəlˌraʊzɪŋ/ &middot; *RAB-uhl-row-zing*
**Русский:** подстрекательский, демагогический
**Type:** compound; also participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rabble-rousing*, *most rabble-rousing*
**Form:** compound — noun + *-ing* participle; a *rabble* is a disorderly crowd

Stirring up the anger of a crowd, usually for political ends.

1. He gave a *rabble-rousing* speech outside the capitol.
2. The paper called her a *rabble-rousing* populist.
3. His second speech was even more *rabble-rousing* than the first.

---

### rabid

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræbɪd/ &middot; *RAB-id*
**Русский:** ярый, фанатичный; бешеный (о животном)
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — the *rabies* sense mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rabid*, *most rabid* (the *fanatical* sense only)

Rabid has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Extreme and fanatical in your opinions or support.** (ярый, фанатичный)

1. He's a *rabid* Yankees fan.
2. The bill faces *rabid* opposition in the Senate.
3. Her most *rabid* supporters lined up at five in the morning.

**2. Of an animal: having rabies.** (бешеный (заражённый бешенством))

4. A *rabid* raccoon bit the neighbor's dog.
5. Animal control caught a *rabid* fox behind the school.

---

### racial

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪʃəl/ &middot; *RAY-shuhl*
**Русский:** расовый
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -al from *race*
**Contrast:** *Racial* is neutral and simply names the category (*racial demographics*). *Racist* is a judgment: it means based on prejudice. *A racial comment* is not the same as *a racist comment*.

Relating to race, or to relations between races.

1. The city studied *racial* disparities in housing.
2. *Racial* profiling is against department policy.
3. They talked about *racial* justice in the classroom.

---

### racist

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪsɪst/ &middot; *RAY-sist*
**Русский:** расистский
**Type:** evaluative; also classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more racist*, *most racist*
**Form:** -ist from *race*
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*He's a racist*). Keep it apart from the neutral *racial*.

Showing or based on prejudice against people of another race.

1. He was fired for a *racist* joke.
2. The policy had a *racist* effect, whatever its intent.
3. His later columns were even more openly *racist*.

---

### racy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪsi/ &middot; *RAY-see*
**Русский:** пикантный, фривольный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *racier*, *raciest*
**Contrast:** Nothing to do with *race* the contest or *race* the group of people — it means mildly sexual.

Slightly shocking, usually because of sexual content.

1. The novel was considered *racy* in 1955.
2. She posted a *racier* photo than usual.
3. It's a *racy* comedy — don't watch it with your parents.

---

### radiant

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪdiənt/ &middot; *RAY-dee-uhnt*
**Русский:** сияющий, лучезарный; лучистый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more radiant*, *most radiant*
**Takes:** *with* + noun — *radiant with joy*

Radiant has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Looking very happy, in a way that shows on your face.** (сияющий, лучезарный)

1. The bride looked *radiant*.
2. She gave us the most *radiant* smile.
3. He was *radiant* with pride at the ceremony.

**2. Sending out light or heat.** (лучистый, излучающий)

4. *Radiant* heat from the floor warms the whole room.
5. The *radiant* glow of the fire filled the cabin.

---

### radical

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrædɪkəl/ &middot; *RAD-i-kuhl*
**Русский:** радикальный, коренной; крайний (полит.)
**Type:** quality; also classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more radical*, *most radical*
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*a radical*). Beyond the political meaning that Russian *радикальный* shares, English *radical* often just means *thorough and far-reaching*: *a radical redesign* is not a political statement.

Radical has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Complete and far-reaching, especially of a change.** (коренной, кардинальный)

1. The company made *radical* changes to the schedule.
2. We need a more *radical* rethink of the budget.
3. She had *radical* surgery and recovered well.

**2. Supporting extreme political or social change.** (радикальный, крайний)

4. She joined a *radical* student group in the seventies.
5. His views got more *radical* over the years.

---

### radio-controlled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪdioʊkənˌtroʊld/ &middot; *RAY-dee-oh-kuhn-trohld*
**Русский:** радиоуправляемый
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + participle; often shortened to *RC*

Operated from a distance by radio signals.

1. He flies a *radio-controlled* plane on weekends.
2. The gate at the end of the driveway is *radio-controlled*.
3. *Radio-controlled* cars were the big gift that Christmas.

---

### radioactive

**Pronunciation:** /ˌreɪdioʊˈæktɪv/ &middot; *ray-dee-oh-AK-tiv*
**Русский:** радиоактивный; политически опасный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more radioactive*, *most radioactive*

Radioactive has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Giving off harmful radiation.** (радиоактивный)

1. The site is still *radioactive* forty years later.
2. They store the *radioactive* waste underground.
3. Some of the soil is more *radioactive* than the air above it.

**2. So controversial that politicians avoid touching it.** (политически опасный, «токсичный»)

4. Social Security is *radioactive* in an election year.
5. The topic became *radioactive* after the scandal.

---

### ragged

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræɡɪd/ &middot; *RAG-id*
**Русский:** рваный, потрёпанный; неровный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ragged*, *most ragged*
**Contrast:** Two syllables: RAG-id, like *rugged* and *wicked*. In *run ragged* it means worn out from overwork: *The twins run me ragged*.

Ragged has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Torn, old and in bad condition.** (рваный, потрёпанный)

1. He wore a *ragged* T-shirt and no shoes.
2. The flag was *ragged* at the edges after the storm.

**2. Uneven and rough, not smooth or controlled.** (неровный, сбивчивый)

3. The team's play was *ragged* in the first quarter.
4. She took a *ragged* breath and kept going.
5. His singing got more *ragged* toward the end of the tour.

---

### raging

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪdʒɪŋ/ &middot; *RAY-jing*
**Русский:** бушующий, яростный; сильный (о боли)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *rage*

Raging has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Very strong and violent, of fire, water or weather.** (бушующий, свирепствующий)

1. A *raging* fire destroyed the barn.
2. They had to cross a *raging* river.

**2. Very intense, of pain, thirst or an argument.** (сильный, ожесточённый)

3. I woke up with a *raging* headache.
4. There's a *raging* debate about the new rules.

---

### rainy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈreɪni/ &middot; *RAY-nee*
**Русский:** дождливый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rainier*, *rainiest*
**Form:** -y from *rain*
**Contrast:** *Save it for a rainy day* means save money for a time when you will need it.

With a lot of rain.

1. It was a cold, *rainy* Tuesday in March.
2. October was *rainier* than usual this year.
3. The whole week is supposed to be *rainy*.

---

### raised

**Pronunciation:** /reɪzd/ &middot; *RAYZD*
**Русский:** приподнятый, возвышающийся
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *raise*
**Contrast:** *Raised voices* means loud and angry ones. *She was raised in Ohio* is the verb, not this adjective.

Higher than the surface around it.

1. We built a *raised* garden bed out of cedar.
2. The band played on a *raised* platform at the end of the room.
3. *Raised* lettering runs across the cover.

---

### rambling

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræmblɪŋ/ &middot; *RAM-bling*
**Русский:** бессвязный, многословный; беспорядочно раскинувшийся
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rambling*, *most rambling*
**Form:** -ing from *ramble*

Rambling has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Long and confused, with no clear point.** (бессвязный, многословный)

1. He left a *rambling* voicemail at two in the morning.
2. The essay is *rambling* and hard to follow.
3. Her second speech was even more *rambling* than the first.

**2. Of a house or garden: spread out in an irregular way.** (беспорядочно раскинувшийся)

4. They bought a *rambling* old farmhouse with six bedrooms.
5. A *rambling* rose covers the back fence.

---

### rambunctious

**Pronunciation:** /ræmˈbʌŋkʃəs/ &middot; *ram-BUHNGK-shuhs*
**Русский:** буйный, неугомонный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rambunctious*, *most rambunctious*
**Contrast:** American English. British speakers say *rumbustious*.

Noisy, full of energy and hard to control, usually of children or animals.

1. Three *rambunctious* boys tore through the kitchen.
2. The puppy gets *rambunctious* right before dinner.
3. The crowd was more *rambunctious* than security expected.

---

### rampant

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræmpənt/ &middot; *RAM-puhnt*
**Русский:** безудержный, свирепствующий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — often after *be* or *run*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rampant*, *most rampant*
**Contrast:** Almost always about something bad that is spreading: *rampant inflation*, *rampant corruption*, never *rampant kindness*.

Spreading fast and out of control.

1. Corruption was more *rampant* in that department than anywhere else in the city.
2. *Rampant* inflation wiped out their savings.
3. Rumors ran *rampant* after the announcement.

---

### ramshackle

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræmˌʃækəl/ &middot; *RAM-shak-uhl*
**Русский:** полуразвалившийся, ветхий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ramshackle*, *most ramshackle*

Badly built or falling apart; of an organization, thrown together and barely working.

1. They spent the summer in a *ramshackle* cabin by the river.
2. The porch looked even more *ramshackle* up close.
3. It's a *ramshackle* operation — nobody knows who's in charge.

---

### rancid

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrænsɪd/ &middot; *RAN-sid*
**Русский:** прогорклый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rancid*, *most rancid*
**Contrast:** *Rancid* is for fats and oils. Milk goes *sour*, meat goes *bad* or is *spoiled*, bread goes *stale*.

Of fat, oil or butter: old, and tasting or smelling unpleasant.

1. The peanut butter went *rancid* in the heat.
2. There's a *rancid* smell coming from that pan.
3. Nuts turn *rancid* faster than most people think.

---

### rancorous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræŋkərəs/ &middot; *RANG-kur-uhs*
**Русский:** злобный, ожесточённый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rancorous*, *most rancorous*
**Form:** -ous from *rancor* — American spelling; British *rancour*

Full of bitter, long-lasting anger.

1. The divorce was long and *rancorous*.
2. A *rancorous* debate split the board in half.
3. Talks grew more *rancorous* with every week that passed.

---

### random

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrændəm/ &middot; *RAN-duhm*
**Русский:** случайный, произвольный; странный (разг.)
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more random*, *most random*
**Contrast:** *At random* is a fixed phrase: *We picked names at random*. The informal *strange* sense is common in speech but out of place in formal writing.

Random has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Chosen or happening with no plan or pattern.** (случайный, произвольный)

1. They called ten *random* names from the list.
2. The attacks look *random*, but the police aren't sure.
3. Each student gets a *random* seat assignment.

**2. Informal: strange, unexpected or out of place.** (странный, ни с того ни с сего)

4. Some *random* guy started talking to me on the bus.
5. That was the most *random* thing he's ever said.

---

### randomized

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrændəˌmaɪzd/ &middot; *RAN-duh-myzd*
**Русский:** рандомизированный, случайным образом распределённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *randomize*
**Contrast:** American spelling *-ized*; British writing often has *randomised*.

Arranged or assigned by chance, especially in research.

1. It was a *randomized* controlled trial with 400 patients.
2. Each group got a *randomized* list of tasks.
3. Students took a *randomized* version of the test.

---

### rank

**Pronunciation:** /ræŋk/ &middot; *RANGK*
**Русский:** явный, отъявленный; вонючий, затхлый
**Type:** emphasizing; also quality
**Position:** both — the *complete* sense only before a noun: *a rank amateur*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rank*, *most rank* (the *smell* sense only)
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*military rank*) and a verb (*The team ranks third*).

Rank has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Complete and total — used before a noun to stress how bad something is.** (явный, отъявленный, сущий)

1. He's a *rank* amateur with a camera.
2. That's *rank* hypocrisy and everyone knows it.
3. It was *rank* stupidity to drive in that storm.

**2. Smelling strong and unpleasant.** (вонючий, затхлый)

4. His gym bag smelled even more *rank* after a week in the car.
5. *Rank* water sat in the bottom of the cooler.

---

### ranked

**Pronunciation:** /ræŋkt/ &middot; *RANGKT*
**Русский:** имеющий место в рейтинге, рейтинговый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — usually in compounds before a noun: *top-ranked*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *rank*; common in *top-ranked*, *highly ranked*, *third-ranked*

Given a position on an official list of the best.

1. She's the third-*ranked* player in the world.
2. Only *ranked* teams make the tournament.
3. He lost to a lower-*ranked* opponent in the second round.

---

### ranking

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræŋkɪŋ/ &middot; *RANG-king*
**Русский:** старший по должности, высокопоставленный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *rank*; usual in *high-ranking*, *top-ranking*
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*the world rankings*). Don't confuse it with *ranked*: the *ranking* officer is the most senior one, a *ranked* player has a number next to their name.

Holding the highest position in a group; most senior.

1. The *ranking* officer took over the scene.
2. She's the *ranking* Democrat on the committee.
3. Several high-*ranking* officials resigned that week.

---

### rapacious

**Pronunciation:** /rəˈpeɪʃəs/ &middot; *ruh-PAY-shuhs*
**Русский:** алчный, хищнический
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rapacious*, *most rapacious*

Greedy, taking as much as possible with no concern for anyone else.

1. *Rapacious* lenders went after older homeowners.
2. He wrote about the *rapacious* side of the timber industry.
3. The company grew more *rapacious* as it lost market share.

---

### rapid

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræpɪd/ &middot; *RAP-id*
**Русский:** быстрый, стремительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rapid*, *most rapid*
**Opposite:** *gradual*
**Contrast:** *Rapid* describes changes and processes. For people, cars and answers, American English says *fast* or *quick*: *a fast runner*, not *a rapid runner*.

Happening fast, especially of change or growth.

1. The town saw *rapid* growth after the plant opened.
2. She made a *rapid* recovery from surgery.
3. Change has been more *rapid* than anyone predicted.

---

### rapt

**Pronunciation:** /ræpt/ &middot; *RAPT*
**Русский:** поглощённый, зачарованный
**Type:** emotion
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Sounds exactly like *wrapped* but is a different word. The set phrase is *rapt attention*.

So interested in something that you notice nothing else.

1. The kids listened with *rapt* attention.
2. She sat *rapt* through the whole concert.
3. A *rapt* audience filled every seat in the hall.

---

### rapturous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræptʃərəs/ &middot; *RAP-chur-uhs*
**Русский:** восторженный
**Type:** emotion
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rapturous*, *most rapturous*
**Form:** -ous from *rapture*

Showing great delight and enthusiasm.

1. The band got a *rapturous* welcome in Chicago.
2. Critics were even more *rapturous* about her second novel.
3. *Rapturous* applause went on for five minutes.

---

### rare

**Pronunciation:** /rɛr/ &middot; *RAIR*
**Русский:** редкий; с кровью (о мясе)
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rarer*, *rarest*
**Takes:** *for* + noun + *to*-infinitive — *It's rare for him to call*
**Opposite:** *common*
**Contrast:** Russian *редкий* also covers hair and combs; English uses *thin* for hair (*thinning hair*) and *fine-toothed* for combs. For meat the scale is *rare*, *medium rare*, *medium*, *medium well*, *well done*.

Rare has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Not often seen, found or done.** (редкий, нечастый)

1. Snow is *rarer* here than it used to be.
2. It's *rare* for him to call on a weekday.
3. The museum owns a *rare* first edition.

**2. Of beef: cooked only a little, still red inside.** (с кровью, полусырой)

4. He orders his steak *rare*.
5. I asked for medium, not *rare*.

---

### rarefied

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛrəfaɪd/ &middot; *RAIR-uh-fyd*
**Русский:** разреженный (о воздухе); элитарный, узкий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rarefied*, *most rarefied*
**Form:** -ed from *rarefy* — to make thinner; note the spelling *rarefied*, not *rarified*

Rarefied has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Belonging to a small, closed world that few people enter.** (элитарный, узкий (о круге))

1. He moved in the *rarefied* world of art collecting.
2. Rare book dealing is an even more *rarefied* world than art collecting.

**2. Of air: thin, with less oxygen than usual.** (разреженный)

3. Climbers struggle in the *rarefied* air above 15,000 feet.
4. The *rarefied* atmosphere makes every step harder.

---

### raring

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛrɪŋ/ &middot; *RAIR-ing*
**Русский:** рвущийся (что-то сделать), горящий желанием
**Type:** emotion
**Position:** predicative only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *to*-infinitive — almost always *raring to go*
**Contrast:** Nearly always in the fixed phrase *raring to go*. It never goes before a noun, and it is not related to *rare*.

Very eager to start something.

1. The team was *raring* to go by kickoff.
2. She's *raring* to get back on the court.
3. We were up at six and *raring* to start.

---

### rash

**Pronunciation:** /ræʃ/ &middot; *RASH*
**Русский:** опрометчивый, необдуманный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rash*, *most rash*
**Takes:** *of* + person + *to*-infinitive — *It was rash of him to quit*
**Contrast:** Also a noun: *a skin rash*, and *a rash of* means a sudden series of something bad (*a rash of break-ins*).

Done or acting too quickly, without thinking about what will happen.

1. Don't make any *rash* decisions while you're angry.
2. It was *rash* of him to quit without another job lined up.
3. It would be even more *rash* to sell the house now.

---

### raspy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræspi/ &middot; *RAS-pee*
**Русский:** хриплый, скрипучий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *raspier*, *raspiest*
**Form:** -y from *rasp* — a rough file, and the sound one makes

Rough and harsh in sound, of a voice or of breathing.

1. He has a *raspy* voice from years of smoking.
2. Her breathing got *raspier* through the night.
3. The singer's *raspy* tone is her signature.

---

### Rastafarian

**Pronunciation:** /ˌræstəˈfɛriən/ &middot; *ras-tuh-FAIR-ee-uhn*
**Русский:** растафарианский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** from *Ras Tafari*, the name of Haile Selassie before he became emperor
**Contrast:** Always capitalized. Also a noun (*a Rastafarian*), often shortened to *Rasta*.

Relating to Rastafari, a religious movement that began in Jamaica in the 1930s.

1. *Rastafarian* beliefs shaped early reggae.
2. She studies *Rastafarian* communities in Jamaica.
3. The red, gold and green stripes are *Rastafarian* colors.

---

### rational

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræʃənəl/ &middot; *RASH-uh-nuhl*
**Русский:** рациональный, разумный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rational*, *most rational*
**Form:** -al from *ratio* — by way of *reason*
**Opposite:** *irrational*
**Contrast:** Don't mix it up with the noun *rationale* (ruh-shuh-NAL), which means the reasoning behind a decision.

Based on clear thinking rather than on emotion; able to think clearly.

1. There's a perfectly *rational* explanation for the noise.
2. Try to be *rational* about the money.
3. Her argument is more *rational* than his, even if it's less popular.

---

### rattled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrætəld/ &middot; *RAT-uhld*
**Русский:** выбитый из колеи, растерянный
**Type:** participial; also emotion
**Position:** both — usually after a verb
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rattled*, *most rattled*
**Form:** -ed from *rattle* — what shakes you shakes your nerves

Nervous and upset after something unsettling.

1. She sounded *rattled* on the phone.
2. The pitcher got *rattled* after the error.
3. He was more *rattled* by the question than he let on.

---

### ratty

**Pronunciation:** /ˈræti/ &middot; *RAT-ee*
**Русский:** потрёпанный, обшарпанный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rattier*, *rattiest*
**Form:** -y from *rat*
**Contrast:** In American English it means *worn out*. In British English *ratty* can mean *irritable*.

Old, worn out and in bad condition.

1. He still wears that *ratty* old hoodie.
2. The couch is even *rattier* than I remembered.
3. *Ratty* carpet covered the stairs.

---

### raucous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɔːkəs/ &middot; *RAW-kuhs*
**Русский:** шумный, горластый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more raucous*, *most raucous*

Loud and rough, especially of a crowd, laughter or a party.

1. A *raucous* crowd filled the bar after the game.
2. There was *raucous* laughter from the back of the room.
3. The party got more *raucous* as the night went on.

---

### raunchy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɔːntʃi/ &middot; *RAWN-chee*
**Русский:** похабный, скабрёзный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *raunchier*, *raunchiest*

Rude and sexual in a crude way.

1. The comedian's set was pretty *raunchy*.
2. Her jokes get *raunchier* after midnight.
3. It's a *raunchy* movie, not one for the family.

---

### ravaged

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrævɪdʒd/ &middot; *RAV-ijd*
**Русский:** опустошённый, разорённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *by* + noun — *ravaged by fire*
**Form:** -ed from *ravage*

Badly damaged or almost destroyed.

1. They flew supplies into the *ravaged* coastal towns.
2. His face was *ravaged* by years of hard living.
3. The *ravaged* forest will take decades to grow back.

---

### ravenous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrævənəs/ &middot; *RAV-uh-nuhs*
**Русский:** голодный как волк, прожорливый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ravenous*, *most ravenous*
**Contrast:** Nothing to do with the bird *raven*. It is much stronger than *hungry* — closer to *starving*.

Extremely hungry.

1. The kids were *ravenous* after practice.
2. By dinnertime they were even more *ravenous*.
3. He ate like a *ravenous* teenager.

---

### raving mad

**Pronunciation:** /ˌreɪvɪŋ ˈmæd/ &middot; *ray-ving MAD*
**Русский:** буйнопомешанный; в бешенстве
**Type:** compound; also emotion
**Position:** predicative only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** fixed phrase — *raving* used as an intensifier + *mad*; the full form is *stark raving mad*
**Contrast:** A fixed phrase, and it goes after a verb, not before a noun. Here *mad* means *crazy*, not *angry* — though people also use the whole phrase for great anger.

Completely crazy, or wild with anger.

1. By the third day of delays he was *raving mad*.
2. You'd have to be *raving mad* to swim in that water.
3. She went stark *raving mad* trying to fix the printer.

---

### ravishing

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrævɪʃɪŋ/ &middot; *RAV-i-shing*
**Русский:** восхитительный, ослепительный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ravishing*, *most ravishing*
**Form:** -ing from *ravish*
**Contrast:** Literary and a little old-fashioned, and usually about beauty. The verb *ravish* has a dark second meaning, but the adjective does not carry it.

Extremely beautiful.

1. She looked *ravishing* in the green dress.
2. The garden is *ravishing* in June.
3. He painted the most *ravishing* sunset I've ever seen.

---

### raw

**Pronunciation:** /rɔː/ &middot; *RAW*
**Русский:** сырой; необработанный; содранный; неприкрытый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rawer*, *rawest*
**Contrast:** *A raw deal* means unfair treatment. *Raw* meat is uncooked; meat that has gone bad is *spoiled*.

Raw has 4 distinct senses.

**1. Not cooked.** (сырой, невареный)

1. Don't eat *raw* cookie dough.
2. The fish is served *raw*, with rice and ginger.

**2. Not yet processed, cleaned up or analyzed.** (необработанный, исходный)

3. The lab sent us the *raw* data.
4. *Raw* materials cost more this year than last.

**3. Of skin: sore, red and painful.** (содранный, саднящий)

5. His hands were *raw* from the rope.
6. My throat felt *raw* after shouting all night.

**4. Honest and full of strong feeling, with nothing softened.** (неприкрытый, откровенный)

7. It's the *rawest* song on the album.
8. The interview was *raw* and uncomfortable to watch.

---

### razor-sharp

**Pronunciation:** /ˌreɪzərˈʃɑːrp/ &middot; *ray-zur-SHAHRP*
**Русский:** острый как бритва; острый (об уме)
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + adjective

Razor-sharp has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Extremely sharp.** (острый как бритва)

1. The knife is *razor-sharp*, so be careful.
2. *Razor-sharp* rocks line that stretch of the shore.

**2. Very quick and precise, of a mind, memory or sense of humor.** (острый, проницательный)

3. At ninety she's still *razor-sharp*.
4. He has a *razor-sharp* memory for names.

---

### reachable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːtʃəbəl/ &middot; *REE-chuh-buhl*
**Русский:** доступный (для связи); достижимый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reachable*, *most reachable*
**Takes:** *by* + noun — *reachable by phone*
**Form:** -able from *reach*
**Opposite:** *unreachable*

Reachable has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Able to be contacted.** (доступный для связи)

1. I'm *reachable* by text all weekend.
2. She's more *reachable* by text than by phone.

**2. Able to be got to or achieved.** (достижимый)

3. The summit is *reachable* in a single day.
4. The goal is ambitious but *reachable*.

---

### reactionary

**Pronunciation:** /riˈækʃəˌnɛri/ &middot; *ree-AK-shuh-nair-ee*
**Русский:** реакционный
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reactionary*, *most reactionary*
**Contrast:** Always a criticism, never a neutral label. It is not the same as *reactive*: a *reactionary* politician wants the past back, a *reactive* one only responds to events.

Opposing social or political change, and wanting things to go back to how they were.

1. The editorial called the ruling *reactionary*.
2. He joined a *reactionary* faction of the party.
3. Her politics grew more *reactionary* with age.

---

### reactive

**Pronunciation:** /riˈæktɪv/ &middot; *ree-AK-tiv*
**Русский:** реагирующий постфактум; химически активный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reactive*, *most reactive*
**Opposite:** *proactive*

Reactive has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Responding to events after they happen instead of planning ahead.** (реагирующий постфактум, несистемный)

1. Management is *reactive* — nothing gets fixed until it breaks.
2. We need to be less *reactive* and more strategic.
3. It was a *reactive* decision made in one afternoon.

**2. In chemistry: easily taking part in chemical reactions.** (химически активный)

4. Sodium is highly *reactive* with water.
5. Store the *reactive* chemicals in a separate cabinet.

---

### readable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːdəbəl/ &middot; *REE-duh-buhl*
**Русский:** интересный, легко читающийся; разборчивый
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more readable*, *most readable*
**Form:** -able from *read*
**Opposite:** *unreadable*
**Contrast:** *Readable* praises the writing; *legible* is only about whether the letters can be made out.

Readable has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Enjoyable and easy to read.** (легко читающийся, увлекательный)

1. It's a dense subject, but the book is very *readable*.
2. She writes the most *readable* science column around.

**2. Clear enough to be read.** (разборчивый, читаемый)

3. The sign is barely *readable* from the road.
4. Make the font bigger so it's *readable* on a phone.

---

### ready-made

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdiˈmeɪd/ &middot; *red-ee-MAYD*
**Русский:** готовый (не на заказ)
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — adjective + participle
**Opposite:** *custom-made*

Already made and ready to use, rather than made to order.

1. We bought a *ready-made* pie crust.
2. He had a *ready-made* excuse waiting.
3. *Ready-made* curtains rarely fit these windows.

---

### ready-to-eat

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdi tə ˈiːt/ &middot; *red-ee-tuh-EET*
**Русский:** готовый к употреблению
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *ready* + *to*-infinitive

Already cooked or prepared, so you can eat it right away.

1. The store sells *ready-to-eat* meals by the register.
2. Pack some *ready-to-eat* snacks for the drive.
3. The salad is washed and *ready-to-eat*.

---

### ready-to-wear

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdi tə ˈwɛr/ &middot; *red-ee-tuh-WAIR*
**Русский:** готовый (об одежде массового пошива)
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *ready* + *to*-infinitive
**Opposite:** *made-to-measure*

Of clothing: made in standard sizes rather than for one person.

1. The designer launched a *ready-to-wear* line last fall.
2. *Ready-to-wear* suits usually need some tailoring anyway.
3. Everything in this part of the store is *ready-to-wear*.

---

### real

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːəl/ &middot; *REE-uhl*
**Русский:** настоящий, реальный; подлинный; сущий
**Type:** quality; also emphasizing
**Position:** both — the emphasizing sense only before a noun: *a real problem*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more real*, *most real*
**Opposite:** *fake*
**Contrast:** *Real good* and *real fast* are informal speech; in writing use the adverb *really*. *For real* is also informal.

Real has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Actually existing, not imagined or invented.** (реальный, существующий)

1. Is the threat *real*, or is it just talk?
2. The dream felt more *real* than the morning did.

**2. Genuine, not a copy or an imitation.** (настоящий, подлинный)

3. The bag is *real* leather.
4. Those aren't *real* diamonds.

**3. Used before a noun to stress how much something is what it is.** (сущий, настоящий (усилительное))

5. The parking downtown is a *real* problem.
6. He can be a *real* pain when he's tired.

---

### real-time

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːəlˌtaɪm/ &middot; *REE-uhl-tym*
**Русский:** работающий в реальном времени
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — adjective + noun
**Contrast:** Hyphenated before a noun, two separate words after a verb: *real-time updates*, but *The data arrives in real time*.

Happening or shown at the same moment as the event itself.

1. The app gives *real-time* traffic updates.
2. We get *real-time* data from the sensors on the bridge.
3. *Real-time* translation is built into the phone.

---

### realistic

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːəˈlɪstɪk/ &middot; *ree-uh-LIS-tik*
**Русский:** реалистичный; трезвый (о взгляде)
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more realistic*, *most realistic*
**Takes:** *about* + noun
**Form:** -istic from *real*
**Opposite:** *unrealistic*

Realistic has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Sensible about what is actually possible.** (трезвый, разумный)

1. Let's set a *realistic* deadline.
2. Be *realistic* about how much you can finish by Friday.
3. Her expectations are more *realistic* now than they were.

**2. Looking or sounding like real life.** (реалистичный, правдоподобный)

4. The special effects are surprisingly *realistic*.
5. He paints in a *realistic* style.

---

### realized

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːəlaɪzd/ &middot; *REE-uh-lyzd*
**Русский:** воплощённый; реализованный (о прибыли)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *realize*; American spelling *-ized*
**Contrast:** *Realize* is usually a verb (*I realized my mistake*). As an adjective it turns up in *fully realized* and in finance, where *realized* gains are ones you have actually taken.

Realized has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Made real and worked out in full.** (воплощённый, законченный)

1. It's a fully *realized* vision of the city in 2100.
2. The characters feel like *realized* people, not sketches.

**2. In finance: actually received or lost, not just on paper.** (реализованный (о прибыли, убытке))

3. You pay tax on *realized* gains, not on paper ones.
4. *Realized* losses can offset those gains.

---

### rear

**Pronunciation:** /rɪr/ &middot; *REER*
**Русский:** задний
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *front*
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*the rear of the building*) and a verb (*to rear children*). As an adjective it never follows a verb: say *The door at the back is locked*, not *The door is rear*.

At or in the back of something.

1. The *rear* door doesn't lock properly.
2. Passengers exit through the *rear* entrance.
3. Check the *rear* tires for wear.

---

### reasonable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːzənəbəl/ &middot; *REE-zuh-nuh-buhl*
**Русский:** разумный; приемлемый; недорогой
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reasonable*, *most reasonable*
**Form:** -able from *reason*
**Opposite:** *unreasonable*

Reasonable has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Fair and sensible.** (разумный, справедливый)

1. That sounds like a *reasonable* request.
2. Be *reasonable* — he's only nine.
3. A more *reasonable* boss would have let it go.

**2. Fairly good or fairly large, though not outstanding.** (неплохой, изрядный)

4. We had a *reasonable* turnout for a rainy night.
5. She has a *reasonable* chance of winning the seat.

**3. Not too expensive.** (недорогой, умеренный (о цене))

6. The hotel was clean and *reasonable*.
7. They charge *reasonable* prices for repairs.

---

### reasoned

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːzənd/ &middot; *REE-zuhnd*
**Русский:** обоснованный, аргументированный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reasoned*, *most reasoned*
**Form:** -ed from *reason*
**Contrast:** *Reasoned* is about the argument — carefully built. *Reasonable* is about the person or the demand — fair and sensible.

Based on careful thinking and clear argument.

1. She wrote a *reasoned* response to the criticism.
2. The judge gave a carefully *reasoned* opinion.
3. We need a more *reasoned* debate about the bill.

---

### reassured

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːəˈʃʊrd/ &middot; *ree-uh-SHUURD*
**Русский:** успокоенный, ободрённый
**Type:** emotion; also participial
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reassured*, *most reassured*
**Takes:** *about* or *by* + noun; *that*-clause
**Form:** -ed — how the person feels
**Contrast:** *reassured* is how you feel; *reassuring* is the thing that causes it: *I felt reassured because her voice was reassuring*.

No longer worried, because someone has taken your fear away.

1. She felt *reassured* after talking to the doctor.
2. Everyone left the meeting *reassured* about their jobs.
3. I was *more reassured* by her calm tone than by anything she actually said.

---

### reassuring

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːəˈʃʊrɪŋ/ &middot; *ree-uh-SHUUR-ing*
**Русский:** успокаивающий, обнадёживающий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reassuring*, *most reassuring*
**Form:** -ing — what causes the feeling
**Opposite:** *alarming*
**Contrast:** The *-ing* form describes the thing that calms you; the *-ed* form describes the person: *a reassuring nurse*, *a reassured patient*.

Making someone feel less worried or afraid.

1. Her voice on the phone was calm and *reassuring*.
2. He gave the crowd a *reassuring* smile.
3. The nurse's explanation was far *more reassuring* than the leaflet.

---

### rebellious

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈbɛljəs/ &middot; *rih-BEL-yuhs*
**Русский:** бунтарский, непокорный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rebellious*, *most rebellious*
**Form:** -ous from *rebel*
**Opposite:** *obedient*

Refusing to obey rules or the people in charge.

1. He was a *rebellious* teenager who skipped class all the time.
2. The students grew *more rebellious* as the new rules piled up.
3. There is something *rebellious* about the way she dresses.

---

### reborn

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːˈbɔːrn/ &middot; *ree-BORN*
**Русский:** возрождённый, заново рождённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** re- + *born*

Changed so completely that it feels like a new life or a new start.

1. After two weeks off, he felt completely *reborn*.
2. The old factory district has been *reborn* as an arts center.
3. The team looked *reborn* in the second half.

---

### recalcitrant

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkælsɪtrənt/ &middot; *rih-KAL-sih-truhnt*
**Русский:** несговорчивый, упрямый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more recalcitrant*, *most recalcitrant*
**Opposite:** *cooperative*

Formal: stubbornly refusing to cooperate or obey.

1. A few *recalcitrant* senators blocked the bill for a month.
2. The mule was *recalcitrant* and would not move.
3. He got *more recalcitrant* with every new rule the school added.

---

### receding

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈsiːdɪŋ/ &middot; *rih-SEE-ding*
**Русский:** отступающий; редеющий (о волосах)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *recede*

Moving back and away from you, or slowly getting smaller.

1. He is self-conscious about his *receding* hairline.
2. We watched the *receding* taillights disappear around the corner.
3. The *receding* floodwaters left mud across the whole street.

---

### received

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈsiːvd/ &middot; *rih-SEEVD*
**Русский:** общепринятый, устоявшийся
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** As an adjective it lives in a few fixed phrases — *received wisdom*, *received opinion*, *received ideas*. In *I received your email* it is just the verb.

Generally accepted by most people, though not necessarily true.

1. The *received* wisdom is that you should never put everything in one stock.
2. She spent her career challenging *received* ideas about diet.
3. That view has hardened into *received* opinion in the field.

---

### recent

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːsənt/ &middot; *REE-suhnt*
**Русский:** недавний, последний
**Type:** age
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more recent*, *most recent*

Happening or made a short time ago.

1. In *recent* years the town has almost doubled in size.
2. Is there a *more recent* version of the report?
3. The damage is *recent* — it happened during last week's storm.

---

### receptive

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈsɛptɪv/ &middot; *rih-SEP-tiv*
**Русский:** восприимчивый, открытый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more receptive*, *most receptive*
**Takes:** *to* + noun or *-ing*
**Opposite:** *unreceptive*

Willing to listen to new ideas and think about them.

1. The boss was surprisingly *receptive* to my idea.
2. Try to pitch it to a *receptive* audience first.
3. Voters are *more receptive* to the message than they were last year.

---

### recessed

**Pronunciation:** /ˈriːsɛst/ &middot; *REE-sest*
**Русский:** утопленный, встроенный в нишу
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *recess*

Set back into a wall, ceiling or surface instead of sticking out.

1. The kitchen has *recessed* lighting in the ceiling.
2. The door handle is *recessed* so nothing catches on it.
3. His eyes were dark and deeply *recessed*.

---

### recessionary

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈsɛʃəˌnɛri/ &middot; *rih-SESH-uh-nair-ee*
**Русский:** рецессионный, связанный со спадом
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ary from *recession*

Belonging to a period when the economy is shrinking.

1. The economy is showing *recessionary* signs again.
2. Hiring freezes are a classic *recessionary* move.
3. Many firms cut travel in the *recessionary* years after 2008.

---

### recessive

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈsɛsɪv/ &middot; *rih-SES-iv*
**Русский:** рецессивный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *dominant*

Of a gene: showing up only when a child gets it from both parents.

1. Blue eyes come from a *recessive* gene.
2. The trait is *recessive*, so both parents have to carry it.
3. Red hair is a *recessive* characteristic.

---

### rechargeable

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːˈtʃɑːrdʒəbəl/ &middot; *ree-CHAR-juh-buhl*
**Русский:** перезаряжаемый, аккумуляторный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** re- + *chargeable*; -able — can be done
**Opposite:** *disposable*

Able to be filled with electricity again and used many times.

1. The flashlight takes *rechargeable* batteries.
2. Is this battery *rechargeable*?
3. We switched to *rechargeable* AAs to save money.

---

### reciprocal

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈsɪprəkəl/ &middot; *rih-SIP-ruh-kuhl*
**Русский:** взаимный, обоюдный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *one-sided*

Given by each side to the other, in both directions.

1. The two countries signed a *reciprocal* trade agreement.
2. Respect between a teacher and a student should be *reciprocal*.
3. Our states have a *reciprocal* deal on driver's licenses.

---

### reckless

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛkləs/ &middot; *REK-luhs*
**Русский:** безрассудный, безответственный
**Type:** quality; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reckless*, *most reckless*
**Takes:** *with* + noun
**Form:** -less from an old verb *reck* — to care
**Opposite:** *careful*

Acting without thinking about the danger or the damage.

1. He was charged with *reckless* driving.
2. It would be *reckless* to spend the whole budget in January.
3. Her second husband was even *more reckless* with money than her first.

---

### reclaimed

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkleɪmd/ &middot; *rih-KLAYMD*
**Русский:** восстановленный; вторично используемый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *reclaim*

Taken back and made usable again, especially old material or land.

1. The table is made of *reclaimed* wood from an old barn.
2. Much of the airport sits on *reclaimed* land.
3. The water is *reclaimed* and used to irrigate the golf course.

---

### reclusive

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkluːsɪv/ &middot; *rih-KLOO-siv*
**Русский:** затворнический, нелюдимый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reclusive*, *most reclusive*
**Opposite:** *sociable*

Living alone and avoiding other people.

1. The *reclusive* author has not given an interview in thirty years.
2. He became *more reclusive* after his wife died.
3. She has grown quite *reclusive* in old age.

---

### recognizable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛkəɡˌnaɪzəbəl/ &middot; *REK-uhg-ny-zuh-buhl*
**Русский:** узнаваемый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more recognizable*, *most recognizable*
**Form:** -able from *recognize*
**Opposite:** *unrecognizable*

Easy to know again when you see or hear it.

1. Her voice is instantly *recognizable*.
2. He wore a hat, but his walk was still *recognizable*.
3. The logo is one of the *most recognizable* in the world.

---

### recognized

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛkəɡnaɪzd/ &middot; *REK-uhg-nyzd*
**Русский:** признанный, общепризнанный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable — usually graded with *widely*: *a widely recognized expert*
**Form:** -ed from *recognize*

Officially accepted, or accepted by everyone as real or good.

1. She is a *recognized* expert on immigration law.
2. The certificate is *recognized* by employers across the country.
3. Burnout is now a *recognized* medical condition.

---

### reconciled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛkənsaɪld/ &middot; *REK-uhn-syld*
**Русский:** смирившийся; помирившийся
**Type:** participial
**Position:** predicative only — after *be*, *become* or *seem*
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reconciled*, *most reconciled*
**Takes:** *to* + noun or *-ing* (accepting); *with* + person (friendly again)
**Form:** -ed from *reconcile*

Reconciled has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Having accepted something unwelcome that cannot be changed.** (смирившийся (с чем-то))

1. She is *reconciled* to living alone.
2. He seems *reconciled* to the fact that he will never play again.
3. I am *more reconciled* to the move than I was in January.

**2. Friendly again after a quarrel.** (помирившийся)

4. The brothers were finally *reconciled* after ten years of silence.
5. She hoped to be *reconciled* with her father before he died.

---

### reconstructive

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːkənˈstrʌktɪv/ &middot; *ree-kuhn-STRUHK-tiv*
**Русский:** восстановительный, реконструктивный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable

Of surgery: rebuilding a part of the body that was damaged.

1. She had *reconstructive* surgery after the accident.
2. He is a *reconstructive* surgeon at the children's hospital.
3. Insurance covered the *reconstructive* work but not the cosmetic part.

---

### record-breaking

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛkərdˌbreɪkɪŋ/ &middot; *REK-urd-bray-king*
**Русский:** рекордный, побивший рекорд
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + participle

Better or bigger than anything of its kind before.

1. She swam a *record-breaking* time in the 200 meters.
2. The movie had a *record-breaking* opening weekend.
3. It was a *record-breaking* heat wave for July.

---

### recorded

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkɔːrdɪd/ &middot; *rih-KOR-did*
**Русский:** записанный, в записи; зафиксированный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *record*
**Opposite:** *live*

Recorded has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Captured on tape, video or a computer so it can be played later.** (записанный, в записи)

1. They played *recorded* music instead of hiring a band.
2. This call may be *recorded* for training purposes.
3. He left a *recorded* message for the whole staff.

**2. Written down in an official record.** (зафиксированный, отмеченный в документах)

4. It was the warmest June in *recorded* history.
5. There is no *recorded* case of the disease in this state.

---

### recoverable

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkʌvərəbəl/ &middot; *rih-KUHV-ur-uh-buhl*
**Русский:** восстановимый; подлежащий возврату
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *recover*
**Opposite:** *unrecoverable*

Able to be got back after being lost, spent or deleted.

1. Deleted files are usually *recoverable* for about thirty days.
2. Only part of the loss is *recoverable* through insurance.
3. The data on the burned drive was not *recoverable*.

---

### recovered

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkʌvərd/ &middot; *rih-KUHV-urd*
**Русский:** выздоровевший; найденный, возвращённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *recover*

Recovered has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Healthy again after an illness or injury.** (выздоровевший, поправившийся)

1. She is fully *recovered* from the surgery.
2. He looks *recovered*, but he still tires by noon.

**2. Found and brought back after being lost or stolen.** (найденный, возвращённый)

3. Police returned the *recovered* jewelry to its owner.
4. The *recovered* files filled three hard drives.

---

### recovering

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkʌvərɪŋ/ &middot; *rih-KUHV-ur-ing*
**Русский:** выздоравливающий; завязавший (об алкоголике)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — after a verb it is the verb *recover*, not an adjective: *She is recovering well*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *recover*
**Contrast:** A *recovering* alcoholic keeps the *-ing* form for life — *recovered* would suggest the problem is over and gone.

Getting better after an illness, an injury or an addiction.

1. He is a *recovering* alcoholic and has been sober for six years.
2. The ward has a quiet lounge for *recovering* patients.
3. She counsels *recovering* addicts at a community center.

---

### recreational

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛkriˈeɪʃənəl/ &middot; *rek-ree-AY-shuh-nuhl*
**Русский:** развлекательный, любительский
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -al from *recreation*

Done for fun in your free time, not for work or medical need.

1. The town is building a new *recreational* center by the lake.
2. *Recreational* marijuana is legal in several states.
3. He plays *recreational* soccer on Sunday mornings.

---

### rectal

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛktəl/ &middot; *REK-tuhl*
**Русский:** ректальный, прямой кишки
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable

Medical: of or through the rectum.

1. The nurse took a *rectal* temperature.
2. He is scheduled for a *rectal* exam next week.
3. The medicine comes as a *rectal* suppository.

---

### recurrent

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkɜːrənt/ &middot; *rih-KUR-uhnt*
**Русский:** повторяющийся, периодический
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ent from *recur*
**Contrast:** *recurrent* and *recurring* mean nearly the same thing; *recurring* is the everyday word and *recurrent* is the one doctors and critics use.

Coming back again and again over a long time.

1. She has a *recurrent* dream about missing a train.
2. Back pain has been a *recurrent* problem for him since the fall.
3. The theme is *recurrent* throughout the novel.

---

### recurring

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈkɜːrɪŋ/ &middot; *rih-KUR-ing*
**Русский:** повторяющийся, регулярный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *recur*

Happening again and again, often on a set schedule.

1. Set up a *recurring* payment so you do not forget the rent.
2. It is a *recurring* joke on the show.
3. She has had *recurring* headaches since the concussion.

---

### recyclable

**Pronunciation:** /ˌriːˈsaɪkləbəl/ &middot; *ree-SY-kluh-buhl*
**Русский:** перерабатываемый, подлежащий переработке
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *recycle*

Able to be treated and used again instead of thrown away.

1. Put all *recyclable* materials in the blue bin.
2. Is this container *recyclable*?
3. Most glass and aluminum is *recyclable*.

---

### red-blooded

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdˈblʌdɪd/ &middot; *red-BLUHD-id*
**Русский:** полнокровный, темпераментный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — adjective + noun + *-ed*
**Contrast:** Usually said of men and often half-joking. It means full of energy and ordinary appetites, not angry or violent.

Full of healthy energy and normal desires.

1. He is a *red-blooded* American teenager.
2. No *red-blooded* fan would miss that game.
3. It is the kind of movie that appeals to *red-blooded* action lovers.

---

### red-carpet

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdˈkɑːrpɪt/ &middot; *red-KAR-pit*
**Русский:** торжественный, по высшему разряду
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — color + noun, from the carpet rolled out for important guests

Grand and ceremonious, the way important guests are welcomed.

1. They gave the visiting team the *red-carpet* treatment.
2. He got a *red-carpet* welcome at the airport.
3. It was a *red-carpet* reception, complete with photographers.

---

### red-faced

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdˈfeɪst/ &middot; *red-FAYST*
**Русский:** красный (от смущения или злости); сконфуженный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — usually intensified with *very* or *increasingly*
**Form:** compound — adjective + noun + *-ed*

Red-faced has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Embarrassed, especially in public.** (сконфуженный, смущённый)

1. The mayor was left *red-faced* when the numbers turned out to be wrong.
2. A *red-faced* official admitted the mistake to reporters.

**2. With a red face from anger, heat or hard effort.** (покрасневший, багровый)

3. He came in *red-faced* and out of breath.
4. The coach was *red-faced* with rage by halftime.

---

### red-handed

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdˈhændɪd/ &middot; *red-HAN-did*
**Русский:** с поличным
**Type:** compound
**Position:** predicative only — almost always after *catch*: *caught red-handed*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — color + noun + *-ed*, from the image of blood on a killer's hands
**Contrast:** It lives in one phrase, *catch someone red-handed*, and never goes before a noun.

In the act of doing something wrong, so there is no denying it.

1. The guard caught him *red-handed* with the cash in his pocket.
2. They were caught *red-handed* copying answers off a phone.
3. If you take it, you will get caught *red-handed*.

---

### red-hot

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdˈhɑːt/ &middot; *red-HAHT*
**Русский:** раскалённый докрасна; очень горячий, злободневный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — color + adjective

Red-hot has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Heated until the metal glows red.** (раскалённый докрасна)

1. He pulled the *red-hot* iron out of the fire with tongs.
2. Do not touch the burner — it is *red-hot*.

**2. Very exciting, very successful or very much in demand right now.** (очень популярный, горячий, злободневный)

3. She is the *red-hot* favorite for the award.
4. The company's stock was *red-hot* all last quarter.

---

### red-letter

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdˈlɛtər/ &middot; *red-LET-ur*
**Русский:** памятный, знаменательный (день)
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only — almost always in *a red-letter day*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — from the red ink that marked feast days on old calendars

Of a day: important and happy, one you will remember.

1. The day she got her citizenship was a *red-letter* day.
2. Graduation is a *red-letter* day for the whole family.
3. Mark it on the calendar — it is a *red-letter* day around here.

---

### reddish

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛdɪʃ/ &middot; *RED-ish*
**Русский:** красноватый, рыжеватый
**Type:** color
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable — the *-ish* ending already means somewhat
**Form:** -ish — somewhat, from *red*

Slightly red, or red mixed with another color.

1. She has *reddish* hair and very pale skin.
2. The sky over the desert turned a *reddish* orange.
3. The soil here is *reddish* and full of clay.

---

### reddish-brown

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɛdɪʃˈbraʊn/ &middot; *red-ish-BROWN*
**Русский:** красно-коричневый
**Type:** color
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — two colors joined with a hyphen

Brown with a clear touch of red in it.

1. The dog has a thick *reddish-brown* coat.
2. The leaves turn a deep *reddish-brown* in late October.
3. The stain on the carpet was *reddish-brown* and would not come out.

---

### redeemable

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈdiːməbəl/ &middot; *rih-DEE-muh-buhl*
**Русский:** подлежащий обмену или погашению
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *for* + what you get; *at* + where you use it
**Form:** -able from *redeem*

Able to be exchanged for goods, money or a service.

1. The coupon is *redeemable* for one free coffee.
2. These points are *redeemable* at any partner hotel.
3. The voucher is *redeemable* in store only.

---

### redeeming

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈdiːmɪŋ/ &middot; *rih-DEE-ming*
**Русский:** искупающий, спасающий (о качестве)
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only — almost always with *quality*, *feature* or *value*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *redeem*

Good enough to make up for everything that is bad about something.

1. The movie's one *redeeming* quality is the soundtrack.
2. He has no *redeeming* features that I can see.
3. The apartment is tiny, but the view is its *redeeming* feature.

---

### redemptive

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈdɛmptɪv/ &middot; *rih-DEMP-tiv*
**Русский:** искупительный, спасительный
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable

Saving someone from guilt or failure, and giving them a second chance.

1. The novel ends on a *redemptive* note.
2. He sees his volunteer work as *redemptive*.
3. There is a *redemptive* quality to the final scene.

---

### redheaded

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɛdˌhɛdɪd/ &middot; *RED-hed-id*
**Русский:** рыжий, рыжеволосый
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — color + noun + *-ed*
**Contrast:** The person is *a redhead* (noun); the adjective keeps the *-ed*: *a redheaded kid*.

Having red or orange hair.

1. A *redheaded* boy answered the door.
2. Both of her kids are *redheaded*.
3. He is the *redheaded* one in the back row.

---

### redoubtable

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈdaʊtəbəl/ &middot; *rih-DOW-tuh-buhl*
**Русский:** грозный, внушающий уважение
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more redoubtable*, *most redoubtable*
**Contrast:** It means formidable and worth respecting. It has nothing to do with *doubt*, even though it looks like it.

Strong and impressive enough to be worth fearing or respecting.

1. She faced a *redoubtable* opponent in the final.
2. The *redoubtable* Mrs. Hayes ran the office for forty years.
3. He proved a *more redoubtable* negotiator than anyone expected.

---

### reduced

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈduːst/ &middot; *rih-DOOST*
**Русский:** сниженный, уменьшенный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *reduce*
**Opposite:** *increased*

Made smaller, lower or cheaper than before.

1. The store sells last season's coats at *reduced* prices.
2. Students pay a *reduced* fare on the city bus.
3. He works a *reduced* schedule while he finishes his degree.

---

### redundant

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈdʌndənt/ &middot; *rih-DUHN-duhnt*
**Русский:** излишний, избыточный; дублирующий
**Type:** evaluative; also classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more redundant*, *most redundant*
**Contrast:** In British English *made redundant* means laid off from a job. Americans do not use it that way — they say *laid off*.

Redundant has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Repeating something that has already been said, and so not needed.** (излишний, избыточный)

1. Saying free gift is *redundant* — a gift is already free.
2. Cut the second paragraph; it is *redundant*.
3. The report gets *more redundant* with every draft.

**2. Built in twice on purpose, as a backup.** (дублирующий, резервный)

4. The plane has *redundant* control systems.
5. Customer data is stored on *redundant* servers in two states.

---

### refined

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈfaɪnd/ &middot; *rih-FYND*
**Русский:** изысканный, утончённый; очищенный, рафинированный
**Type:** participial; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more refined*, *most refined*
**Form:** -ed from *refine*
**Opposite:** *crude*

Refined has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Polite, cultured and in good taste.** (изысканный, утончённый)

1. She has beautifully *refined* manners.
2. His taste in music is *more refined* than mine.

**2. Made pure by industrial processing.** (очищенный, рафинированный)

3. Try to cut back on *refined* sugar and white flour.
4. *Refined* oil is shipped by rail from the coast.

**3. Improved by many small careful changes.** (доработанный, отточенный)

5. The second version of the design is far *more refined*.
6. We use a *refined* version of the original formula.

---

### reflective

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈflɛktɪv/ &middot; *rih-FLEK-tiv*
**Русский:** задумчивый; светоотражающий; отражающий положение дел
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more reflective*, *most reflective*
**Takes:** *of* + noun, in the showing sense
**Form:** -ive from *reflect*

Reflective has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Thinking quietly and seriously about things.** (задумчивый, вдумчивый)

1. He was in a *reflective* mood all evening.
2. The last chapter is calm and *reflective*.
3. She has grown *more reflective* with age.

**2. Throwing back light, so it can be seen in the dark.** (светоотражающий)

4. Runners should wear a *reflective* vest at night.
5. The jacket has *reflective* stripes down the sleeves.

**3. Showing what something is really like, or being a sign of it.** (отражающий, показательный)

6. The test scores are *reflective* of the whole district.
7. His answer was *reflective* of a much deeper problem.

---

### reflexive

**Pronunciation:** /rɪˈflɛksɪv/ &middot; *rih-FLEK-siv*
**Русский:** рефлекторный, инстинктивный; возвратный (грамм.)
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable

Reflexive has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Done automatically, without stopping to think.** (рефлекторный, инстинктивный)

1. His *reflexive* answer was no, and he regretted it later.
2. She has a *reflexive* distrust of salespeople.
