**Русский:** ритуальный; механически повторяющийся
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ritualistic*, *most ritualistic*
**Form:** -istic from *ritual*

Done in a fixed, repeated way, like a ceremony.

1. The team has a *ritualistic* warm-up before every game.
2. His hand-washing had become *ritualistic*.
3. There is something *ritualistic* about Sunday dinner at their house.

---

### ritzy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɪtsi/ &middot; *RIT-see*
**Русский:** шикарный, роскошный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *ritzier*, *ritziest*
**Form:** from the Ritz hotels
**Contrast:** Informal and a little dated. *Fancy* or *upscale* is what people say now.

Expensive and fashionable.

1. They stayed at a *ritzy* hotel downtown.
2. It is the *ritziest* neighborhood in the city.
3. The place is too *ritzy* for jeans.

---

### riveting

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɪvətɪŋ/ &middot; *RIV-uh-ting*
**Русский:** захватывающий, приковывающий внимание
**Type:** participial; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more riveting*, *most riveting*
**Form:** -ing from *rivet* 'to fasten with a metal pin'
**Opposite:** *dull*

So interesting that you cannot look away.

1. The trial made for *riveting* television.
2. Her testimony was absolutely *riveting*.
3. It is a *riveting* account of the escape.

---

### roaring

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɔːrɪŋ/ &middot; *ROR-ing*
**Русский:** ревущий, гудящий; оглушительный, бурный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *roar*

Roaring has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Making a loud, deep, continuous sound.** (ревущий, гудящий)

1. We sat by a *roaring* fire.
2. A *roaring* crowd greeted the team at the airport.

**2. Very great or lively — in a few fixed phrases.** (оглушительный, бурный)

3. The bake sale was a *roaring* success.
4. The 1920s are still called the *Roaring* Twenties.

---

### roast

**Pronunciation:** /roʊst/ &middot; *ROHST*
**Русский:** жареный в духовке, запечённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Before a noun, dish names keep the old form: *roast beef*, *roast chicken*, *roast potatoes*. Everywhere else say *roasted*: *The potatoes were roasted, not fried*.

Cooked in an oven or over a fire — in the names of dishes.

1. We had *roast* beef and potatoes.
2. She ordered the *roast* chicken.
3. *Roast* duck is the house specialty.

---

### roasted

**Pronunciation:** /ˈroʊstɪd/ &middot; *ROH-stid*
**Русский:** жареный, запечённый; обжаренный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *roast*
**Contrast:** The everyday form. Only the old dish names use plain *roast* (*roast beef*).

Cooked or browned with dry heat, in an oven or over a fire.

1. *Roasted* vegetables are the easiest side dish there is.
2. The peppers were *roasted* and then peeled.
3. Try the salad with *roasted* almonds.

---

### robotic

**Pronunciation:** /roʊˈbɑːtɪk/ &middot; *roh-BAH-tik*
**Русский:** роботизированный; механический, как у робота
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more robotic*, *most robotic*
**Form:** -ic from *robot*

Robotic has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Belonging to or done by robots.** (роботизированный, робототехнический)

1. The factory uses a *robotic* arm for welding.
2. He had *robotic* surgery on his knee.

**2. Stiff and without feeling, like a machine.** (механический, деревянный)

3. Her voice on the phone was flat and *robotic*.
4. He gave a *robotic* answer and moved on to the next caller.

---

### robust

**Pronunciation:** /roʊˈbʌst/ &middot; *roh-BUHST*
**Русский:** крепкий, здоровый; прочный, устойчивый; насыщенный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more robust*, *most robust*

Robust has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Strong and healthy.** (крепкий, здоровый)

1. He is eighty and still *robust*.
2. These plants are *robust* enough to survive a frost.

**2. Strong and working well, of a system, an economy or a debate.** (прочный, устойчивый, активный)

3. We need a more *robust* password policy.
4. Sales were *robust* over the holidays.

**3. Strong and full, of a taste or smell.** (насыщенный, крепкий (о вкусе))

5. It is a *robust* red wine that stands up to steak.
6. He likes his coffee dark and *robust*.

---

### rock-bottom

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrɑːkˈbɑːtəm/ &middot; *rahk-BAH-tuhm*
**Русский:** самый низкий, минимальный (о цене)
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + noun
**Contrast:** As two words it is a noun phrase: *He hit rock bottom last year*.

As low as it can possibly go — almost always of prices.

1. They are selling tickets at *rock-bottom* prices.
2. Gas hit *rock-bottom* rates that winter.
3. We got a *rock-bottom* deal on the car.

---

### rocky

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɑːki/ &middot; *RAH-kee*
**Русский:** скалистый, каменистый; трудный, шаткий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rockier*, *rockiest*
**Form:** -y from *rock*

Rocky has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Covered with rocks, or made of rock.** (скалистый, каменистый)

1. They hiked along a *rocky* beach.
2. The trail gets *rockier* near the top.

**2. Full of problems and not at all steady.** (трудный, шаткий)

3. The marriage got off to a *rocky* start.
4. It has been a *rocky* year for the company.

---

### rolling

**Pronunciation:** /ˈroʊlɪŋ/ &middot; *ROH-ling*
**Русский:** холмистый, волнистый; поэтапный, скользящий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *roll*

Rolling has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Rising and falling in long gentle slopes, of land.** (холмистый, волнистый)

1. We drove past *rolling* hills and old farms.
2. The house sits on forty acres of *rolling* farmland.

**2. Happening in stages, one area or group at a time.** (поэтапный, скользящий)

3. The city had *rolling* blackouts all August.
4. They announced a *rolling* schedule of layoffs.

---

### Roman

**Pronunciation:** /ˈroʊmən/ &middot; *ROH-muhn*
**Русский:** римский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Also a noun for a person (*the Romans*). With a small letter, *roman* is the plain upright type you are reading now, as opposed to *italic*.

Of ancient Rome, or of the city of Rome today.

1. They found a *Roman* coin in the field.
2. *Roman* roads still run across parts of Europe.
3. The chapters are numbered with *Roman* numerals.

---

### Roman Catholic

**Pronunciation:** /ˌroʊmən ˈkæθlɪk/ &middot; *roh-muhn KATH-lik*
**Русский:** римско-католический
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*He's a Roman Catholic*). In everyday speech people usually just say *Catholic*.

Belonging to the Christian church that has the Pope at its head.

1. She was raised *Roman Catholic*.
2. They were married in a *Roman Catholic* church.
3. The hospital is *Roman Catholic* and was founded by nuns.

---

### Romance

**Pronunciation:** /roʊˈmæns/ &middot; *roh-MANS*
**Русский:** романский (о языках)
**Type:** origin; also classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** A different word from the noun *romance* 'a love story', and it is always capitalized.

Of the languages that grew out of Latin — French, Spanish, Italian, Portuguese and Romanian.

1. Spanish and Italian are *Romance* languages.
2. She studies *Romance* philology.
3. The *Romance* languages all give nouns a gender.

---

### Romanian

**Pronunciation:** /roʊˈmeɪniən/ &middot; *roh-MAY-nee-uhn*
**Русский:** румынский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Also a noun: the people (*a Romanian*) and the language (*She speaks Romanian*).

From Romania, or connected with Romania and its language.

1. He is a *Romanian* filmmaker living in Berlin.
2. We had dinner at a *Romanian* restaurant.
3. Her grandparents are *Romanian*.

---

### romantic

**Pronunciation:** /roʊˈmæntɪk/ &middot; *roh-MAN-tik*
**Русский:** романтический, любовный; романтичный; идеализированный
**Type:** quality; also emotion
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more romantic*, *most romantic*
**Contrast:** Also a noun (*a hopeless romantic*). Capital *Romantic* means the poets and composers of the early 1800s.

Romantic has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Having to do with love between people.** (романтический, любовный)

1. They are in a *romantic* relationship.
2. He planned a *romantic* dinner for their anniversary.

**2. Beautiful in a way that makes you think of love.** (романтичный)

3. Venice at night is impossibly *romantic*.
4. It is the most *romantic* restaurant in town.

**3. Idealistic, and not based on how things really are.** (идеализированный, оторванный от реальности)

5. He has a *romantic* view of the old West.
6. That is a *romantic* notion of what soldiers actually do.

---

### roomy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈruːmi/ &middot; *ROO-mee*
**Русский:** просторный, вместительный
**Type:** size
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *roomier*, *roomiest*
**Form:** -y from *room* 'space'
**Opposite:** *cramped*

Having plenty of space inside.

1. The car is surprisingly *roomy* in back.
2. Their new place is *roomier* than the old one.
3. I want a *roomy* bag for the trip.

---

### rooted

**Pronunciation:** /ˈruːtɪd/ &middot; *ROO-tid*
**Русский:** укоренённый, уходящий корнями; приросший к месту
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — before a noun with an adverb: *a deeply rooted belief*
**Comparison:** not gradable — the adverb carries the degree: *deeply rooted*, *firmly rooted*
**Takes:** *in* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *root*; also the compound *deep-rooted*
**Contrast:** *Rooted for* is a different verb: to *root for* a team means to cheer for it.

Rooted has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Coming from something and firmly fixed in it.** (укоренённый, уходящий корнями)

1. The custom is *rooted* in the nineteenth century.
2. His fear of water is *rooted* in one bad afternoon.

**2. Unable to move, as if fixed to the ground.** (приросший к месту)

3. I stood *rooted* to the spot.
4. She was *rooted* in place by the noise.

---

### rose-colored

**Pronunciation:** /ˌroʊz ˈkʌlərd/ &middot; *rohz-KUH-lurd*
**Русский:** розовый; идеализированный
**Type:** compound; also evaluative
**Position:** attributive only — almost always in *rose-colored glasses*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + noun + *-ed*
**Contrast:** American spelling is *rose-colored*; British writing has *rose-coloured* or *rose-tinted*.

Rose-colored has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Seeing things as better than they really are.** (идеализирующий, розовый)

1. He looks back at the nineties through *rose-colored* glasses.
2. Take off the *rose-colored* glasses and look at the numbers.
3. The report gives a *rose-colored* picture of the economy.

**2. Pink, the color of a rose.** (розового цвета)

4. She picked a *rose-colored* fabric for the curtains.
5. The invitations came on *rose-colored* card stock.

---

### rosy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈroʊzi/ &middot; *ROH-zee*
**Русский:** румяный, розовый; радужный
**Type:** color; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rosier*, *rosiest*
**Form:** -y from *rose*
**Contrast:** *Rosy* drops the *e* of *rose*, and the *y* becomes *i* in comparison: *rosier*, *rosiest*.

Rosy has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Pink and healthy-looking, usually about skin.** (румяный)

1. The baby had *rosy* cheeks after the walk.
2. Her face was *rosy* from the cold.
3. His cheeks looked *rosier* after a week at the beach.

**2. Hopeful; promising a good future.** (радужный, оптимистичный)

4. The company painted a *rosy* picture of next year.
5. Things are not as *rosy* as the press release suggests.

---

### rosy-cheeked

**Pronunciation:** /ˌroʊzi ˈtʃiːkt/ &middot; *roh-zee-CHEEKT*
**Русский:** румяный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — adjective + noun + *-ed*

Having pink, healthy-looking cheeks.

1. A *rosy-cheeked* boy opened the door.
2. The choir was full of *rosy-cheeked* kids in scarves.
3. She played a *rosy-cheeked* farm girl in the movie.

---

### rotary

**Pronunciation:** /ˈroʊtəri/ &middot; *ROH-tuh-ree*
**Русский:** вращательный, роторный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ary from Latin *rota* 'wheel'
**Contrast:** In New England *a rotary* is also a noun — a traffic circle. Elsewhere in the US people say *traffic circle* or *roundabout*.

Turning around a center point; working by turning.

1. The old phone had a *rotary* dial.
2. A *rotary* engine has no pistons going up and down.
3. The bakery bakes everything in a *rotary* oven.

---

### rotten

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɑːtən/ &middot; *RAH-tuhn*
**Русский:** гнилой, испорченный; отвратительный
**Type:** quality; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rotten*, *most rotten*
**Opposite:** *fresh*
**Contrast:** *Rotten* fits food, wood and teeth. Milk that has gone bad is *spoiled* or *sour*, not *rotten*.

Rotten has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Decayed and no longer good.** (гнилой, тухлый)

1. The apples at the bottom of the bag were *rotten*.
2. The whole porch is *rotten* underneath.
3. Something smells *rotten* in the fridge.

**2. Very bad or unpleasant.** (отвратительный, паршивый)

4. We had *rotten* luck with the weather.
5. That was a *rotten* thing to say.
6. I feel *rotten* about canceling on you.

---

### rough

**Pronunciation:** /rʌf/ &middot; *RUHF*
**Русский:** шероховатый; тяжёлый; приблизительный; грубый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rougher*, *roughest*
**Opposite:** *smooth*
**Contrast:** The *gh* is pronounced /f/: *rough* rhymes with *stuff*. The adverb *roughly* also means 'approximately': *roughly ten miles*.

Rough has 4 distinct senses.

**1. Not smooth or even to the touch.** (шероховатый, неровный)

1. The wall was *rough* to the touch.
2. My hands get *rough* in the winter.
3. This road is *rougher* than the one we took.

**2. Difficult or unpleasant to live through.** (тяжёлый, трудный)

4. She's had a *rough* year.
5. It was a *rough* flight.

**3. Approximate; not finished or exact.** (приблизительный, черновой)

6. Give me a *rough* estimate.
7. This is just a *rough* draft.

**4. Using force; not gentle or safe.** (грубый, буйный)

8. Don't be too *rough* with the puppy.
9. The neighborhood used to be pretty *rough*.

---

### rough-and-tumble

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrʌf ən ˈtʌmbəl/ &middot; *ruhf-uhn-TUHM-buhl*
**Русский:** грубоватый, беспорядочный; жёсткий
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — adjective + *and* + verb

Rough and disorderly, but usually without real harm.

1. *Rough-and-tumble* play is normal for puppies.
2. He grew up in a *rough-and-tumble* neighborhood.
3. She loves the *rough-and-tumble* world of city politics.

---

### round-the-clock

**Pronunciation:** /ˌraʊnd ðə ˈklɑːk/ &middot; *rownd-thuh-KLAHK*
**Русский:** круглосуточный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — preposition phrase used as one word
**Contrast:** Before a noun it is the adjective *round-the-clock care*; after a verb use the adverb *around the clock*: *They worked around the clock*.

Going on all day and all night without stopping.

1. The patient needs *round-the-clock* care.
2. There was *round-the-clock* coverage of the trial.
3. The plant runs on a *round-the-clock* schedule.

---

### roundabout

**Pronunciation:** /ˈraʊndəbaʊt/ &middot; *ROWN-duh-bowt*
**Русский:** окольный, обходной; уклончивый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** In Britain *a roundabout* is a noun — a traffic circle. As an adjective meaning 'indirect' it is normal American English.

Not direct — going the long way, or said in an indirect way.

1. We took a *roundabout* route to avoid the highway.
2. He told me in a *roundabout* way that I hadn't gotten the job.
3. That's a *roundabout* explanation for something very simple.

---

### rounded

**Pronunciation:** /ˈraʊndɪd/ &middot; *ROWN-did*
**Русский:** закруглённый; всесторонний
**Type:** shape; also participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rounded*, *most rounded*
**Form:** -ed from *round*

Rounded has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Curved, with no sharp corners or edges.** (закруглённый)

1. The table has *rounded* corners.
2. Her handwriting is soft and *rounded*.
3. Sand the edges until they're *rounded*.

**2. Complete and balanced, covering every side of something.** (всесторонний, разносторонний)

4. The school aims to give kids a *rounded* education.
5. She wanted a *more rounded* view of the problem.

---

### rousing

**Pronunciation:** /ˈraʊzɪŋ/ &middot; *ROW-zing*
**Русский:** воодушевляющий, зажигательный
**Type:** participial; also evaluative
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rousing*, *most rousing*
**Form:** -ing from *rouse*

Making people excited and enthusiastic.

1. The coach gave a *rousing* speech at halftime.
2. The band ended with a *rousing* version of the anthem.
3. The bill passed to *rousing* applause.

---

### routine

**Pronunciation:** /ruːˈtiːn/ &middot; *roo-TEEN*
**Русский:** обычный, плановый, рутинный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun: *a routine checkup*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** The stress is on the second syllable: *roo-TEEN*. *A routine* is also a noun — a fixed order of doing things, or a dance or comedy act.

Done regularly as normal procedure; ordinary.

1. It was a *routine* traffic stop.
2. She's in for *routine* surgery on Thursday.
3. There is nothing *routine* about this case.

---

### roving

**Pronunciation:** /ˈroʊvɪŋ/ &middot; *ROH-ving*
**Русский:** странствующий, разъездной
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *rove*
**Contrast:** *A roving eye* is a set phrase for someone who keeps looking at other people romantically.

Moving from place to place instead of staying in one.

1. She works as a *roving* reporter for the network.
2. A *roving* band of musicians played between the tables.
3. The museum sends a *roving* exhibit to small towns.

---

### rowdy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈraʊdi/ &middot; *ROW-dee*
**Русский:** буйный, шумный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rowdier*, *rowdiest*
**Opposite:** *quiet*

Noisy and rough, in a way that can cause trouble.

1. A *rowdy* group of fans filled the bar.
2. The crowd got *rowdier* after the third quarter.
3. It was a *rowdy* wedding, in the best way.

---

### royal

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrɔɪəl/ &middot; *ROY-uhl*
**Русский:** королевский; (разг.) полный, страшный
**Type:** classifying; also emphasizing
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Royal* means 'of a king or queen'; *regal* means 'looking like royalty'. A *royal pain* has nothing to do with kings — it just means a very big nuisance.

Royal has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Belonging to or connected with a king or queen.** (королевский)

1. The *royal* family spent the summer in Scotland.
2. The palace is still a *royal* residence.
3. She received a *royal* pardon.

**2. Complete, total — used before a few words to make them stronger.** (полный, страшный)

4. Parking downtown is a *royal* pain.
5. He made a *royal* mess of the kitchen.

---

### rubbery

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌbəri/ &middot; *RUH-buh-ree*
**Русский:** резиновый (на ощупь), жёсткий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rubbery*, *most rubbery*
**Form:** -y from *rubber*
**Contrast:** A *rubber* mat is made of rubber; *rubbery* chicken only feels like it.

Tough and bendy like rubber, usually said about badly cooked food.

1. The calamari was *rubbery*.
2. Microwaved eggs come out *rubbery*.
3. He cut into a *rubbery* piece of steak.

---

### ruddy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌdi/ &middot; *RUH-dee*
**Русский:** румяный, красноватый
**Type:** color
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *ruddier*, *ruddiest*

Red and healthy-looking, said about skin.

1. He had a *ruddy* face from working outdoors.
2. Her complexion was *ruddy* after the hike.
3. His cheeks looked *ruddier* than usual.

---

### rudimentary

**Pronunciation:** /ˌruːdɪˈmɛntəri/ &middot; *roo-dih-MEN-tuh-ree*
**Русский:** элементарный, зачаточный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rudimentary*, *most rudimentary*
**Opposite:** *advanced*

Very basic — only the first and simplest level.

1. He has a *rudimentary* knowledge of Spanish.
2. The clinic has only *rudimentary* equipment.
3. Our plan is still pretty *rudimentary*.

---

### ruffled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌfəld/ &middot; *RUH-fuhld*
**Русский:** взъерошенный; встревоженный; с оборками
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ruffled*, *most ruffled*
**Form:** -ed from *ruffle*
**Contrast:** *Unruffled* is the common opposite of the second sense: *She stayed unruffled through the whole meeting*.

Ruffled has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Pushed out of place so it is no longer smooth.** (взъерошенный, растрёпанный)

1. The bird sat there with *ruffled* feathers.
2. His hair was *ruffled* from the wind.

**2. Slightly upset or annoyed.** (встревоженный, задетый)

3. She looked a little *ruffled* by the question.
4. Nothing gets him *ruffled*.

**3. Made with ruffles — strips of gathered cloth.** (с оборками)

5. She wore a white *ruffled* blouse.
6. The dress has a *ruffled* hem.

---

### rugged

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌɡɪd/ &middot; *RUH-gid*
**Русский:** суровый, неровный; крепкий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rugged*, *most rugged*
**Contrast:** The *-ed* is always /ɪd/ and makes a second syllable: *RUH-gid*, never *ruhgd*.

Rugged has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Rough, rocky and hard to travel across.** (неровный, труднопроходимый)

1. The *rugged* coastline is hard to reach by car.
2. We hiked through *rugged* terrain for six hours.

**2. Strong and built to survive hard use.** (крепкий, прочный)

3. These boots are *rugged* enough for winter work.
4. The laptop comes in a *rugged* case.

**3. Of a man's face: strong-featured in an attractive way.** (мужественный (о лице))

5. He has a *rugged*, weather-beaten face.
6. The ad was looking for a *rugged* type.

---

### ruined

**Pronunciation:** /ˈruːɪnd/ &middot; *ROO-ind*
**Русский:** разрушенный; испорченный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *ruin*

Ruined has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Destroyed, with only broken remains left.** (разрушенный)

1. We walked around a *ruined* castle above the river.
2. The *ruined* church is still standing after the fire.

**2. Spoiled so badly that it is no good any more.** (испорченный)

3. My shoes were *ruined* by the road salt.
4. The whole evening was *ruined* by one phone call.

---

### ruinous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈruːɪnəs/ &middot; *ROO-ih-nuhs*
**Русский:** губительный, разорительный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ruinous*, *most ruinous*
**Form:** -ous from *ruin*

Causing very serious damage, especially to money or a business.

1. The lawsuit was *ruinous* for the family business.
2. They borrowed at *ruinous* interest rates.
3. A second bad harvest would be *ruinous*.

---

### ruling

**Pronunciation:** /ˈruːlɪŋ/ &middot; *ROO-ling*
**Русский:** правящий, господствующий
**Type:** participial; also classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *rule*
**Contrast:** *A ruling* is also a noun — a judge's or an official's decision.

Having power over a country or an organization.

1. The *ruling* party lost its majority.
2. Members of the *ruling* class sent their children abroad.
3. The *ruling* coalition fell apart in March.

---

### rumpled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌmpəld/ &middot; *RUHM-puhld*
**Русский:** мятый, помятый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rumpled*, *most rumpled*
**Form:** -ed from *rumple*
**Opposite:** *neat*

Wrinkled and messy, usually said about clothes or bedding.

1. He showed up in a *rumpled* suit.
2. The sheets were *rumpled* where the cat had slept.
3. Her jacket looked *rumpled* after the flight.

---

### run-down

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrʌn ˈdaʊn/ &middot; *ruhn-DOWN*
**Русский:** запущенный, обветшалый; измотанный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more run-down*, *most run-down*
**Form:** compound — verb + particle
**Contrast:** *A rundown*, written as one word with the stress on *run*, is a noun meaning a quick summary: *Give me the rundown*.

Run-down has 2 distinct senses.

**1. In bad condition because nobody has taken care of it.** (запущенный, обветшалый)

1. They bought a *run-down* house on the edge of town.
2. The whole block looks *run-down*.

**2. Tired and slightly unwell.** (измотанный, ослабленный)

3. I've been feeling *run-down* since the trip.
4. You look *run-down* — take a day off.

---

### run-of-the-mill

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrʌn əv ðə ˈmɪl/ &middot; *ruhn-uhv-thuh-MIL*
**Русский:** заурядный, обычный
**Type:** compound; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *exceptional*

Ordinary, with nothing special about it.

1. It's a *run-of-the-mill* office job.
2. The food was *run-of-the-mill* diner food.
3. This is no *run-of-the-mill* thunderstorm.

---

### run-on

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌn ɑːn/ &middot; *RUHN-ahn*
**Русский:** слитный, без нужной пунктуации (о предложении)
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — verb + particle
**Contrast:** A grammar term: a *run-on sentence* pushes two sentences together with no period and no conjunction.

Of a sentence: made of two sentences joined without the right punctuation.

1. Her essay was full of *run-on* sentences.
2. Fix the *run-on* sentence in the second paragraph.
3. A comma splice is one kind of *run-on* error.

---

### runaway

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌnəweɪ/ &middot; *RUH-nuh-way*
**Русский:** беглый; неуправляемый; безоговорочный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *A runaway* is also a noun — a child or teenager who has left home.

Runaway has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Having escaped or left home without permission.** (беглый, сбежавший)

1. The shelter takes in *runaway* teens.
2. A *runaway* horse blocked the road for an hour.

**2. Out of control and impossible to stop.** (неуправляемый)

3. A *runaway* truck hit the barrier on the hill.
4. *Runaway* inflation wiped out their savings.

**3. Won very easily; extremely successful.** (безоговорочный, оглушительный)

5. The movie was a *runaway* hit.
6. She won the primary in a *runaway* victory.

---

### running

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌnɪŋ/ &middot; *RUH-ning*
**Русский:** проточный; непрерывный, текущий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ing from *run*
**Contrast:** In *running shoes* and *running track*, *running* is a noun used as a modifier, not an adjective. *Three days running* is an adverb meaning 'in a row'.

Running has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Of water: flowing, especially from a tap.** (проточный)

1. The cabin has no *running* water.
2. Rinse the rice under *running* water.

**2. Continuing over a long time, with new parts added.** (непрерывный, текущий)

3. It's a *running* joke in our family.
4. Keep a *running* total as you shop.
5. They have a *running* argument about the thermostat.

---

### runny

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌni/ &middot; *RUH-nee*
**Русский:** жидкий; сопливый, слезящийся
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *runnier*, *runniest*

Runny has 2 distinct senses.

**1. More liquid than it should be.** (жидкий, текучий)

1. The sauce came out *runny*.
2. I like my eggs a little *runny*.
3. This batter is *runnier* than the recipe says.

**2. Of a nose or eyes: producing liquid because of a cold or an allergy.** (сопливый, слезящийся)

4. She has a *runny* nose and a cough.
5. My eyes get *runny* every spring.

---

### rural

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʊrəl/ &middot; *RUUR-uhl*
**Русский:** сельский
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Opposite:** *urban*
**Contrast:** *Rural* is a neutral fact of geography; *rustic* adds old-fashioned charm. A city restaurant can look *rustic*, never *rural*.

In or about the countryside, away from cities.

1. She grew up in *rural* Kansas.
2. *Rural* hospitals are closing all over the state.
3. The area north of the lake is still very *rural*.

---

### rushed

**Pronunciation:** /rʌʃt/ &middot; *RUHSHT*
**Русский:** поспешный; в спешке
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rushed*, *most rushed*
**Form:** -ed from *rush*

Rushed has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Done too fast, without enough time to do it well.** (поспешный, скомканный)

1. The ending of the movie felt *rushed*.
2. We ate a *rushed* breakfast and ran for the train.

**2. Of a person: forced to hurry.** (торопящийся, в спешке)

3. I felt *rushed* the whole morning.
4. Don't let them make you feel *rushed*.

---

### Russian Orthodox

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrʌʃən ˈɔːrθədɑːks/ &middot; *ruh-shuhn OR-thuh-dahks*
**Русский:** русский православный
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** Russian *православный* on its own is *Orthodox* or *Eastern Orthodox*; *Russian Orthodox* names the Russian church in particular. Both words take a capital letter.

Belonging to the Orthodox church of Russia.

1. They were married in a *Russian Orthodox* church in Brooklyn.
2. His grandmother is *Russian Orthodox*.
3. *Russian Orthodox* Christmas falls on January 7.

---

### Russian-speaking

**Pronunciation:** /ˌrʌʃən ˈspiːkɪŋ/ &middot; *ruh-shuhn-SPEE-king*
**Русский:** русскоязычный
**Type:** compound; also origin
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + *-ing* participle
**Contrast:** *Russian-speaking* is about the language, not the country: a *Russian-speaking* family may be from Kazakhstan or Israel. After a verb say *They speak Russian*.

Using Russian as an everyday language.

1. The clinic is looking for a *Russian-speaking* nurse.
2. There is a large *Russian-speaking* community in Brighton Beach.
3. She joined a *Russian-speaking* book club.

---

### rustic

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌstɪk/ &middot; *RUH-stik*
**Русский:** деревенский, простой (в хорошем смысле)
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more rustic*, *most rustic*
**Contrast:** *Rustic* is a compliment about style — plain and country-like. *Rural* is only about location.

Plain and old-fashioned in a country way that people find charming.

1. The restaurant has a *rustic* look — bare wood and iron lamps.
2. We rented a *rustic* cabin with a wood stove.
3. The bread is dense and *rustic*.

---

### rusty

**Pronunciation:** /ˈrʌsti/ &middot; *RUH-stee*
**Русский:** ржавый; подзабытый (о навыке)
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *rustier*, *rustiest*
**Form:** -y from *rust*

Rusty has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Covered with rust.** (ржавый)

1. A *rusty* nail was sticking out of the board.
2. The gate gets *rustier* every year.

**2. Of a skill: weaker than it was because you have not practiced.** (подзабытый, утраченный без практики)

3. My Spanish is pretty *rusty*.
4. He's *rusty* after two years off the ice.

---

### ruthless

**Pronunciation:** /ˈruːθləs/ &middot; *ROOTH-luhs*
**Русский:** безжалостный, беспощадный
**Type:** quality; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more ruthless*, *most ruthless*
**Form:** -less — literally 'without ruth', an old word for pity
**Opposite:** *merciful*

Willing to hurt people to get what you want; showing no pity.

1. He was *ruthless* with anyone who slowed the project down.
2. It's a *ruthless* business.
3. She made a *ruthless* cut to the budget.

---

### Rwandan

**Pronunciation:** /ruːˈɑːndən/ &middot; *roo-AHN-duhn*
**Русский:** руандийский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *A Rwandan* is also the noun for a person from Rwanda.

From or relating to Rwanda.

1. The *Rwandan* team qualified for the tournament.
2. He teaches *Rwandan* history at the community college.
3. Her husband is *Rwandan*.

---

### saber-toothed

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪbər tuːθt/ &middot; *SAY-bur-tootht*
**Русский:** саблезубый
**Type:** compound
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — noun + noun + *-ed*
**Contrast:** American spelling is *saber*; British writing has *sabre-toothed*.

Having very long, curved upper teeth — said of certain extinct cats.

1. The museum has a *saber-toothed* cat skeleton.
2. Kids always want to see the *saber-toothed* tiger.
3. *Saber-toothed* predators died out about ten thousand years ago.

---

### saccharine

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsækərɪn/ &middot; *SAK-uh-rin*
**Русский:** приторный, слащавый
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more saccharine*, *most saccharine*
**Contrast:** The adjective ends in *-e*: *saccharine*. *Saccharin*, with no *e*, is the noun for the artificial sweetener.

So sweet and sentimental that it feels fake.

1. The movie ends on a *saccharine* note.
2. He sang it in a *saccharine* voice.
3. The card's message was a little *saccharine* for my taste.

---

### sacred

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪkrɪd/ &middot; *SAY-krid*
**Русский:** священный, святой
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sacred*, *most sacred*
**Takes:** *to* + person or group (*sacred to the Lakota*)
**Contrast:** The *-ed* is /ɪd/ and makes a second syllable: *SAY-krid*. *Sacred* covers religion and deep respect; *holy* is stronger and almost always religious.

Sacred has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Connected with religion and treated with religious respect.** (священный, святой)

1. The mountain is *sacred* to several tribes.
2. The choir sang *sacred* music at the service.

**2. Too important to change, break or joke about.** (неприкосновенный, святой)

3. Sunday dinner is *sacred* in this family.
4. Nothing was *sacred* in his comedy.

---

### sacrificial

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsækrɪˈfɪʃəl/ &middot; *sak-rih-FISH-uhl*
**Русский:** жертвенный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ial from *sacrifice*
**Contrast:** *A sacrificial lamb* is a set phrase for the person blamed or given up so that others are protected.

Offered up or given up, especially so that something else is saved.

1. The manager became the *sacrificial* lamb after a bad season.
2. Ancient texts describe a *sacrificial* altar on the hill.
3. He made a *sacrificial* move to protect his queen.

---

### sacrilegious

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsækrəˈlɪdʒəs/ &middot; *sak-ruh-LIJ-uhs*
**Русский:** кощунственный, святотатственный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sacrilegious*, *most sacrilegious*
**Contrast:** Watch the spelling: *sacrilegious* has *-rileg-*, not *-relig-*, even though it is about religion.

Treating something holy — or something people deeply respect — with disrespect.

1. Wearing shoes inside the temple would be *sacrilegious*.
2. Putting ketchup on it is practically *sacrilegious* in Chicago.
3. The church called the painting *sacrilegious*.

---

### sacrosanct

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsækroʊsæŋkt/ &middot; *SAK-roh-sangkt*
**Русский:** неприкосновенный, священный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable

So important that nobody is allowed to change or question it.

1. In this office, Friday afternoons are *sacrosanct*.
2. The contract is not *sacrosanct* — we can renegotiate.
3. He treats his grandmother's recipe as *sacrosanct*.

---

### sadistic

**Pronunciation:** /səˈdɪstɪk/ &middot; *suh-DIS-tik*
**Русский:** садистский
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sadistic*, *most sadistic*
**Form:** -ic from *sadist*

Enjoying other people's pain or fear.

1. The coach had a *sadistic* sense of humor.
2. It felt *sadistic* to make them run in that heat.
3. He plays a *sadistic* villain in the new series.

---

### safe and sound

**Pronunciation:** /ˌseɪf ən ˈsaʊnd/ &middot; *sayf-uhn-SOWND*
**Русский:** цел и невредим
**Type:** quality
**Position:** predicative only — after *be*, *arrive*, *get home* and similar verbs
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** A fixed phrase, always in this order and only after a verb: *They got home safe and sound*, never *a safe and sound child*.

Not hurt and no longer in danger, after something risky.

1. The hikers were found *safe and sound* the next morning.
2. Call me when you get there *safe and sound*.
3. All three kids came back *safe and sound*.

---

### sage

**Pronunciation:** /seɪdʒ/ &middot; *SAYJ*
**Русский:** мудрый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Sage* is also a noun — the herb, and a word for a wise old person. As an adjective it sounds slightly literary; everyday English says *wise*.

Wise, in a calm and experienced way.

1. She gave me some *sage* advice about buying a house.
2. The column is full of *sage* observations.
3. A *sage* nod was all the answer I got.

---

### sagging

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsæɡɪŋ/ &middot; *SAG-ing*
**Русский:** провисающий, обвисший; падающий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sagging*, *most sagging*
**Form:** -ing from *sag*

Sagging has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Hanging down in the middle under its own weight.** (провисающий, обвисший)

1. We had to replace the *sagging* porch roof.
2. The old mattress was *sagging* in the middle.

**2. Falling or getting weaker, about sales, prices or spirits.** (падающий, слабеющий)

3. *Sagging* sales forced the store to close.
4. They tried to lift the team's *sagging* morale.

---

### Saharan

**Pronunciation:** /səˈhɛrən/ &middot; *suh-HAIR-uhn*
**Русский:** сахарский
**Type:** origin; also classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -an from *Sahara*
**Contrast:** *Sub-Saharan Africa* means the part of the continent south of the desert, and is far more common in the news than *Saharan* alone.

Of or from the Sahara desert.

1. A cloud of *Saharan* dust reached Florida last summer.
2. The plant survives in *Saharan* conditions.
3. *Saharan* nights can be surprisingly cold.

---

### saintly

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪntli/ &middot; *SAYNT-lee*
**Русский:** святой, безгрешный, ангельский
**Type:** quality; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more saintly*, *most saintly*
**Form:** -ly adjective from *saint*
**Contrast:** *Saintly* ends in *-ly* but is an adjective, not an adverb: *saintly patience*, never *He smiled saintly*.

Extremely good, patient and forgiving.

1. She had *saintly* patience with the twins.
2. Nobody is *saintly* at six in the morning.
3. He was *saintly* about the whole mess.

---

### salacious

**Pronunciation:** /səˈleɪʃəs/ &middot; *suh-LAY-shuhs*
**Русский:** непристойный, скабрёзный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more salacious*, *most salacious*
**Contrast:** *Salacious* is about sexual detail served up for gossip. It is stronger and far more disapproving than *racy*.

Full of sexual detail, in a way meant to shock or excite.

1. The tabloid ran the *salacious* details on page one.
2. The book was banned as *salacious* in 1928.
3. He kept the story alive with *salacious* rumors.

---

### salaried

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsælərid/ &middot; *SAL-uh-reed*
**Русский:** получающий оклад, на окладе
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *salary*
**Contrast:** A *salaried* worker is paid a fixed amount per year; an *hourly* worker is paid by the hour. In the US the difference decides who gets overtime.

Paid a fixed yearly salary rather than by the hour.

1. *Salaried* employees don't punch a clock.
2. She moved from an hourly job to a *salaried* position.
3. The company has about two hundred *salaried* staff.

---

### salient

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪliənt/ &middot; *SAY-lee-uhnt*
**Русский:** заметный, существенный, главный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more salient*, *most salient*
**Contrast:** Nothing to do with salt. It means 'standing out as important', and is most often met in *the salient points*.

Standing out as the most noticeable or important.

1. Let me go over the *salient* points of the contract.
2. The *most salient* fact is that nobody was home.
3. Her age is not *salient* here — her experience is.

---

### saline

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪliːn/ &middot; *SAY-leen*
**Русский:** солевой, соляной
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Saline* is a technical word from medicine and chemistry. Food that tastes of salt is *salty*, never *saline*.

Containing salt; made of a salt solution.

1. The nurse flushed the line with *saline* solution.
2. Wash the wound with a *saline* rinse.
3. The lake is highly *saline*.

---

### salted

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɔːltɪd/ &middot; *SAWL-tid*
**Русский:** солёный, подсоленный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *salt*
**Contrast:** *Salted* means salt was added — *salted butter*. *Salty* describes the taste: *The soup is too salty*.

With salt added, for taste or to keep it from spoiling.

1. Use *salted* butter for the topping.
2. A bowl of *salted* peanuts came with the drinks.
3. *Salted* caramel is on every dessert menu now.

---

### salty

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɔːlti/ &middot; *SAWL-tee*
**Русский:** солёный; (разг.) обиженный, злой
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *saltier*, *saltiest*
**Takes:** *about* + noun or *-ing* — in the slang sense
**Form:** -y from *salt*
**Contrast:** The slang sense is everywhere in American speech: *Why are you so salty?* means 'Why are you so annoyed?'

Salty has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Tasting of salt, or containing a lot of it.** (солёный)

1. The soup is too *salty*.
2. Ocean air feels *salty* on your skin.
3. This batch is *saltier* than the last one.

**2. Annoyed or bitter about something, in American slang.** (обиженный, раздражённый)

4. He's still *salty* about not getting picked.
5. Don't get *salty* with me — I warned you.

---

### Salvadoran

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsælvəˈdɔːrən/ &middot; *sal-vuh-DOR-uhn*
**Русский:** сальвадорский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Salvadoran* is the usual American form; *Salvadorian* also appears. The country's name keeps the article: *El Salvador*.

From or relating to El Salvador.

1. There's a great *Salvadoran* bakery on the corner.
2. She's *Salvadoran* on her mother's side.
3. *Salvadoran* pupusas are worth the drive.

---

### salvageable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsælvɪdʒəbəl/ &middot; *SAL-vih-juh-buhl*
**Русский:** поддающийся спасению, годный к ремонту
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -able from *salvage*
**Opposite:** *unsalvageable*

Still able to be saved, repaired or rescued.

1. Only the engine was *salvageable* after the fire.
2. Is the friendship still *salvageable*?
3. They sorted the wet books into *salvageable* and ruined piles.

---

### same

**Pronunciation:** /seɪm/ &middot; *SAYM*
**Русский:** тот же самый, одинаковый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — always with *the*, *this* or *that*: *the same street*, *They look the same*
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Takes:** *as* + noun or clause (*the same as mine*)
**Opposite:** *different*
**Contrast:** Always *the same*, never *a same*. Compare with *as*, not *like* and never *than*: *the same as mine*. Russian *такой же* and *одинаковый* are both *the same*: *Our phones are the same*.

Same has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Not another one; exactly this one and no other.** (тот же самый)

1. We've lived on the *same* street for twenty years.
2. She asked me the *same* question twice.
3. Your jacket is the *same* as mine.

**2. Not changed from before.** (прежний, неизменный)

4. The town looks the *same* as it did in 1990.
5. He's still the *same* old Dave.

---

### Samoan

**Pronunciation:** /səˈmoʊən/ &middot; *suh-MOH-uhn*
**Русский:** самоанский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *A Samoan* is also the noun for a person, and *Samoan* is the name of the language.

From or relating to Samoa or American Samoa.

1. The team's best lineman is *Samoan*.
2. We went to a *Samoan* church service on the island.
3. She teaches *Samoan* dance on Saturdays.

---

### sanctimonious

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsæŋktɪˈmoʊniəs/ &middot; *sangk-tih-MOH-nee-uhs*
**Русский:** ханжеский, лицемерно-праведный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sanctimonious*, *most sanctimonious*
**Contrast:** *Sanctimonious* is always an insult — it means acting morally better than everyone else. The neutral words are *devout* and *religious*.

Acting as if you are morally better than other people.

1. Spare me the *sanctimonious* lecture about recycling.
2. He gets *sanctimonious* whenever the topic comes up.
3. The editorial struck a *sanctimonious* tone.

---

### sandy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsændi/ &middot; *SAN-dee*
**Русский:** песчаный; песочного цвета
**Type:** quality; also color
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *sandier*, *sandiest*
**Form:** -y from *sand*

Sandy has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Covered with sand, or made mostly of sand.** (песчаный)

1. We found a long *sandy* beach south of town.
2. The soil here is *sandy* and dries out fast.
3. The path got *sandier* closer to the dunes.

**2. Of hair: light yellowish brown.** (песочного цвета, светло-русый)

4. He has *sandy* hair and freckles.
5. Her eyebrows are *sandy*, almost blond.

---

### sane

**Pronunciation:** /seɪn/ &middot; *SAYN*
**Русский:** в здравом уме; разумный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *saner*, *sanest*
**Opposite:** *insane*
**Contrast:** The noun is *sanity*. In everyday speech *insane* often just means 'crazy' or even 'amazing': *That concert was insane*.

Sane has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Mentally healthy; not mentally ill.** (в здравом уме, вменяемый)

1. The court found him *sane* enough to stand trial.
2. Long walks are what keeps me *sane*.

**2. Sensible and reasonable.** (разумный, здравый)

3. Let's set a *sane* deadline for once.
4. A *saner* approach would be to test it first.

---

### sanguine

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsæŋɡwɪn/ &middot; *SANG-gwin*
**Русский:** оптимистичный, бодрый
**Type:** emotion; also quality
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sanguine*, *most sanguine*
**Takes:** *about* + noun
**Contrast:** A formal word. Everyday English says *optimistic* or *hopeful*.

Cheerfully confident that things will turn out well.

1. She's *sanguine* about the company's chances.
2. Analysts are less *sanguine* than they were in March.
3. He took a *sanguine* view of the delay.

---

### sanitary

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsænɪtɛri/ &middot; *SAN-ih-tair-ee*
**Русский:** санитарный; гигиеничный
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sanitary*, *most sanitary*
**Opposite:** *unsanitary*
**Contrast:** Russian *санитарный* matches only the technical sense. English also uses *sanitary* to mean 'clean enough to be safe': *That counter isn't sanitary*.

Sanitary has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Having to do with keeping places clean and free of disease.** (санитарный)

1. The kitchen failed a *sanitary* inspection.
2. *Sanitary* conditions in the camp were poor.

**2. Clean enough to be safe.** (гигиеничный)

3. Eating off that counter isn't very *sanitary*.
4. They keep the prep area *sanitary*.

---

### sanitized

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsænɪtaɪzd/ &middot; *SAN-ih-tyzd*
**Русский:** продезинфицированный; приглаженный, смягчённый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sanitized*, *most sanitized*
**Form:** -ed from *sanitize*
**Contrast:** American spelling is *-ized*; British writing has *sanitised*.

Sanitized has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Cleaned thoroughly so that germs are killed.** (продезинфицированный)

1. All the equipment is *sanitized* between classes.
2. The tray came out *sanitized* and still warm.

**2. With the unpleasant or embarrassing parts taken out.** (приглаженный, смягчённый)

3. The textbook gives a *sanitized* version of the war.
4. It was a *sanitized* retelling for children.

---

### sappy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsæpi/ &middot; *SAP-ee*
**Русский:** слащавый, сентиментальный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *sappier*, *sappiest*
**Contrast:** An American informal word; the British equivalent is *soppy*. It teases rather than attacks.

Too sentimental, in a silly and slightly embarrassing way.

1. We watched a *sappy* Christmas movie.
2. The card was *sappy*, but I kept it.
3. His toast got *sappier* with every glass of wine.

---

### sarcastic

**Pronunciation:** /sɑːrˈkæstɪk/ &middot; *sahr-KAS-tik*
**Русский:** саркастический, язвительный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sarcastic*, *most sarcastic*
**Form:** -ic from *sarcasm*
**Contrast:** *Sarcastic* is saying the opposite of what you mean in order to mock someone. *Ironic* is wider and usually aimed at no one.

Saying the opposite of what you mean, to mock or criticize.

1. 'Great job,' he said, *sarcastic* as ever.
2. Don't be *sarcastic* with your teacher.
3. She made a *sarcastic* comment about my parking.

---

### sardonic

**Pronunciation:** /sɑːrˈdɑːnɪk/ &middot; *sahr-DAH-nik*
**Русский:** сардонический, мрачно-насмешливый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more sardonic*, *most sardonic*
**Contrast:** *Sardonic* is darker and quieter than *sarcastic* — a *sardonic* smile mocks without a word being said.

Mocking in a dark, unamused way.

1. He gave a *sardonic* smile and went back to his coffee.
2. The narrator has a *sardonic* sense of humor.
3. Her tone was dry and *sardonic*.

---

### sartorial

**Pronunciation:** /sɑːrˈtɔːriəl/ &middot; *sahr-TOR-ee-uhl*
**Русский:** портновский, относящийся к одежде
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** A formal, often playful word. It describes choices, style and splendor — never a person directly: *his sartorial taste*, not *a sartorial man*.

Having to do with clothes and the way someone dresses.

1. His *sartorial* choices got more attention than his speech.
2. The piece is about *sartorial* trends in menswear.
3. She arrived in full *sartorial* splendor.

---

### sassy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsæsi/ &middot; *SAS-ee*
**Русский:** дерзкий, бойкий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *sassier*, *sassiest*
**Contrast:** An American word. About a child it is a complaint; about an adult or a style it is usually a compliment.

Sassy has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Cheeky and rude, especially about answering back.** (дерзкий, нахальный)

1. Don't get *sassy* with your grandmother.
2. He was a *sassy* five-year-old.

**2. Lively, bold and confident, often about style.** (бойкий, задорный)

3. She showed up in a *sassy* red dress.
4. The ad copy is *sassier* than anything they've run before.

---

### satanic

**Pronunciation:** /səˈtænɪk/ &middot; *suh-TAN-ik*
**Русский:** сатанинский
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more satanic*, *most satanic*
**Form:** -ic from *Satan*

Of the devil; evil in a religious sense.

1. The town panicked over rumors of a *satanic* cult.
2. The band leaned into *satanic* imagery.
3. He called the whole scheme *satanic*.

---

### satirical

**Pronunciation:** /səˈtɪrɪkəl/ &middot; *suh-TIR-ih-kuhl*
**Русский:** сатирический
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more satirical*, *most satirical*
**Form:** -ical from *satire*
**Contrast:** *Satiric* is a rarer form of the same word. Do not confuse it with *satisfactory* or with *sarcastic*, which is about a person's tone, not a work.

Using humor and exaggeration to criticize people or institutions.

1. The magazine runs a *satirical* column every week.
2. It's a *satirical* novel about Wall Street.
3. The piece is *satirical*, not a real news report.

---

### satisfactory

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsætɪsˈfæktəri/ &middot; *sat-is-FAK-tuh-ree*
**Русский:** удовлетворительный, приемлемый
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more satisfactory*, *most satisfactory*
**Opposite:** *unsatisfactory*
**Contrast:** *Satisfactory* means 'good enough', not 'good' — a *satisfactory* grade is barely passing. *Satisfying* is about pleasure: *a satisfying meal*.

Good enough for the purpose, but no better than that.

1. The repair work was *satisfactory*.
2. We need a *satisfactory* explanation by Friday.
3. Her condition is listed as *satisfactory*.

---

### saturated

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsætʃəreɪtɪd/ &middot; *SACH-uh-ray-tid*
**Русский:** насыщенный; пропитанный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more saturated*, *most saturated*
**Takes:** *with* or *in* + noun
**Form:** -ed from *saturate*

Saturated has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Completely soaked through.** (пропитанный, промокший насквозь)

1. My jacket was *saturated* by the time I got home.
2. The ground is *saturated* after three days of rain.

**2. Holding as much as it possibly can.** (насыщенный)

3. The market is *saturated* with delivery apps.
4. Butter is high in *saturated* fat.
5. The photo has deep, *saturated* colors.

---

### saucy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɔːsi/ &middot; *SAW-see*
**Русский:** дерзкий; фривольный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *saucier*, *sauciest*
**Contrast:** Nothing to do with sauce on food. It is a slightly old-fashioned, playful word for 'cheeky' or 'a little risqué'.

Bold and cheeky, often in a flirty way.

1. She sent him a *saucy* postcard from Miami.
2. The show is famous for its *saucy* jokes.
3. His answer was a little *saucier* than the judge liked.

---

### Saudi

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsaʊdi/ &middot; *SOW-dee*
**Русский:** саудовский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Saudi* alone is the usual adjective; *Saudi Arabian* is also correct. *A Saudi* is the noun for a person.

From or relating to Saudi Arabia.

1. The *Saudi* delegation arrived on Tuesday.
2. He works for a *Saudi* airline.
3. Most of the students in the program are *Saudi*.

---

### sauteed

**Pronunciation:** /soʊˈteɪd/ &middot; *soh-TAYD*
**Русский:** обжаренный на сковороде
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *sauté*, borrowed from French
**Contrast:** Often written with the accent: *sautéed*. It means fried quickly in a little fat, not deep-fried.

Cooked quickly in a pan with a little butter or oil.

1. The steak came with *sauteed* mushrooms.
2. Add the *sauteed* onions to the pot.
3. She served *sauteed* spinach with lemon.

---

### savage

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsævɪdʒ/ &middot; *SAV-ij*
**Русский:** жестокий, свирепый; (сленг) беспощадный
**Type:** quality; also evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more savage*, *most savage*
**Contrast:** Calling people *savage* in the old sense of 'primitive' is offensive today. The living uses are 'very violent' and, in slang, 'brutally blunt'.

Savage has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Very violent or fierce.** (жестокий, свирепый)

1. The dog gave him a *savage* bite.
2. It was a *savage* storm, even for February.

**2. Extremely harsh, about criticism.** (уничтожающий, резкий)

3. The review was *savage*.
4. She wrote a *savage* takedown of the proposal.

**3. Brutally blunt in a way people find funny, in slang.** (беспощадный, дерзкий)

5. His reply was *savage*.
6. That comeback was straight-up *savage*.

---

### savory

**Pronunciation:** /ˈseɪvəri/ &middot; *SAY-vuh-ree*
**Русский:** несладкий, солоноватый; пикантный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more savory*, *most savory*
**Opposite:** *sweet*
**Contrast:** American spelling *savory*; British *savoury*. *Not savory* about a person or a business means shady: *a less than savory character*.

Savory has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Salty or spicy rather than sweet.** (несладкий, солёный)

1. Do you want something sweet or *savory* for breakfast?
2. The bakery makes *savory* hand pies.

**2. Tasting good in a rich, salty way.** (пикантный, насыщенный)

3. The broth was deep and *savory*.
4. Mushrooms add a *savory* note to the sauce.

---

### sawed-off

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsɔːd ˈɔːf/ &middot; *sawd-AWF*
**Русский:** обрезанный, с укороченным стволом
**Type:** compound; also participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — participle + particle
**Contrast:** American *sawed-off*; British *sawn-off*. Used of a person it is an informal, rude way of saying 'short'.

Cut short with a saw, especially of a shotgun.

1. Police recovered a *sawed-off* shotgun from the trunk.
2. He kept a *sawed-off* broom handle behind the counter.
3. The workbench stands on *sawed-off* table legs.

---

### Saxon

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsæksən/ &middot; *SAK-suhn*
**Русский:** саксонский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** In American English *Anglo-Saxon* is far more common; *Saxon* on its own is mostly historical.

Of the Saxons, a Germanic people who settled in England, or of Saxony in Germany.

1. The village church has a *Saxon* tower.
2. He wrote his thesis on *Saxon* law.
3. The porcelain is *Saxon*, from the Meissen works.

---

### scalable

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskeɪləbəl/ &middot; *SKAY-luh-buhl*
**Русский:** масштабируемый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scalable*, *most scalable*
**Form:** -able from *scale*
**Contrast:** Common in business and technology: something *scalable* can grow much bigger without being rebuilt.

Able to grow much larger without breaking or being redesigned.

1. The model isn't *scalable* beyond a few hundred users.
2. They wanted a *scalable* business, not a boutique.
3. We picked a *more scalable* database this time.

---

### scaly

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskeɪli/ &middot; *SKAY-lee*
**Русский:** чешуйчатый; шелушащийся
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scalier*, *scaliest*
**Form:** -y from *scale*
**Contrast:** Spelled *scaly*, with no *e* before the *-y*.

Covered with scales, or with dry, flaking skin.

1. The lizard's *scaly* back caught the light.
2. My elbows get *scaly* in the winter.
3. His skin was *scalier* than the doctor expected.

---

### scandalous

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskændələs/ &middot; *SKAN-duh-luhs*
**Русский:** скандальный, возмутительный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scandalous*, *most scandalous*
**Form:** -ous from *scandal*
**Contrast:** Russian *скандальный* can mean simply 'quarrelsome'; English *scandalous* always involves shock or disgrace. A noisy neighbor is *difficult*, not *scandalous*.

Shocking and morally wrong; causing public outrage.

1. The memo revealed *scandalous* spending.
2. It's *scandalous* that the building is still unsafe.
3. She wrote a book about the family's *scandalous* past.

---

### Scandinavian

**Pronunciation:** /ˌskændɪˈneɪviən/ &middot; *skan-dih-NAY-vee-uhn*
**Русский:** скандинавский
**Type:** origin
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Scandinavian* usually means Denmark, Norway and Sweden. *Nordic* is wider and takes in Finland and Iceland too.

From or relating to Scandinavia.

1. The store sells *Scandinavian* furniture.
2. Her grandparents were *Scandinavian*.
3. He's taking a *Scandinavian* literature class.

---

### scant

**Pronunciation:** /skænt/ &middot; *SKANT*
**Русский:** скудный, едва достаточный
**Type:** quantity
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Scant* goes straight before a noun, often before a number: *a scant two weeks*. After a verb use *scarce* or *not enough*.

Scant has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Barely any; much less than is needed.** (скудный, ничтожный)

1. There is *scant* evidence for either theory.
2. The report got *scant* attention from the press.

**2. Just a little under the amount named.** (неполный, с небольшим недобором)

3. Add a *scant* teaspoon of salt.
4. We finished in a *scant* three hours.

---

### scanty

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskænti/ &middot; *SKAN-tee*
**Русский:** скудный; очень открытый (об одежде)
**Type:** quantity
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scantier*, *scantiest*
**Contrast:** About information *scanty* is close to *scant*, but *scant* only stands before a noun. About clothing only *scanty* works.

Scanty has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Too small in amount.** (скудный, недостаточный)

1. The evidence is *scanty* at best.
2. They lived on *scanty* rations that winter.

**2. Of clothing: covering very little of the body.** (открытый, куцый (об одежде))

3. She wore a *scanty* costume for the number.
4. The outfits got *scantier* as the show went on.

---

### scarce

**Pronunciation:** /skɛrs/ &middot; *SKAIRS*
**Русский:** дефицитный, редкий
**Type:** quantity
**Position:** both — mostly after a verb
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scarcer*, *scarcest*
**Opposite:** *plentiful*
**Contrast:** *Scarce* is about supply — there is not enough of it. *Rare* is about how seldom something occurs: *a rare bird*, but *scarce housing*. The adverb *scarcely* means 'hardly'.

Not available in the amount people need; hard to get.

1. Affordable apartments are *scarce* downtown.
2. Water became *scarcer* every week of the drought.
3. Good news has been *scarce* this year.

---

### scarlet

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskɑːrlɪt/ &middot; *SKAHR-lit*
**Русский:** алый, ярко-красный
**Type:** color
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Contrast:** *Scarlet* is a bright red leaning to orange; *crimson* leans to purple. *Scarlet fever* is a children's illness.

Bright, strong red.

1. She wore a *scarlet* coat to the parade.
2. The maples turn *scarlet* in October.
3. The book's cover is plain *scarlet*.

---

### scarred

**Pronunciation:** /skɑːrd/ &middot; *SKAHRD*
**Русский:** покрытый шрамами; травмированный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scarred*, *most scarred*
**Takes:** *by* + noun (*scarred by the war*)
**Form:** -ed from *scar*, with the *r* doubled
**Contrast:** Do not confuse *scarred* with *scared* (испуганный). They sound different too: *SKAHRD* against *SKAIRD*.

Scarred has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Marked with scars.** (покрытый шрамами)

1. His hands were *scarred* from years of kitchen work.
2. The old table is *scarred* with knife marks.

**2. Emotionally damaged by something painful.** (травмированный)

3. She was *scarred* by the divorce.
4. A whole generation was *scarred* by the war.

---

### scary

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskɛri/ &middot; *SKAIR-ee*
**Русский:** страшный, пугающий
**Type:** emotion; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scarier*, *scariest*
**Form:** -y from *scare*
**Contrast:** *Scary* is the thing that frightens you; *scared* is how you feel. Russian *страшный* covers both: *a scary movie* leaves you *scared*.

Frightening.

1. That was the *scariest* part of the ride.
2. It's *scary* how fast kids grow.
3. We watched a *scary* movie with all the lights on.

---

### scathing

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskeɪðɪŋ/ &middot; *SKAY-thing*
**Русский:** уничтожающий, резкий (о критике)
**Type:** participial; also evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scathing*, *most scathing*
**Form:** -ing from *scathe*, an old verb meaning 'to harm'
**Contrast:** Almost always about words: *a scathing review*, *scathing criticism*. People are not usually called *scathing*.

Extremely harsh in its criticism.

1. The Times ran a *scathing* review.
2. She sent a *scathing* email to the whole department.
3. His report was *more scathing* than anyone expected.

---

### scatterbrained

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskætərbreɪnd/ &middot; *SKAT-ur-braynd*
**Русский:** рассеянный, несобранный
**Type:** compound
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scatterbrained*, *most scatterbrained*
**Form:** compound — verb + noun + *-ed*

Forgetful and disorganized.

1. I've been so *scatterbrained* since the baby came.
2. He's a brilliant but *scatterbrained* professor.
3. She left the keys in the door again — totally *scatterbrained*.

---

### scattered

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskætərd/ &middot; *SKAT-urd*
**Русский:** разбросанный; местами; рассеянный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scattered*, *most scattered*
**Takes:** *across*, *over* or *around* + place
**Form:** -ed from *scatter*

Scattered has 3 distinct senses.

**1. Spread out over an area instead of being together.** (разбросанный, рассеянный)

1. Toys were *scattered* all over the floor.
2. Her family is *scattered* across three states.

**2. Happening here and there rather than everywhere, about weather and outages.** (местами, отдельный)

3. *Scattered* showers are expected this afternoon.
4. There were *scattered* power outages overnight.

**3. Of a person: unable to keep track of things.** (рассеянный, несобранный)

5. I'm a little *scattered* today — I didn't sleep.
6. He sounded *scattered* on the phone.

---

### scattershot

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskætərʃɑːt/ &middot; *SKAT-ur-shaht*
**Русский:** хаотичный, бессистемный
**Type:** compound; also evaluative
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scattershot*, *most scattershot*
**Form:** compound — verb + noun, from the way shotgun pellets spread out
**Contrast:** An American word, and a criticism: it says an effort had no focus, not that it was fast.

Done in a broad, unfocused way, with no real plan.

1. Their marketing is *scattershot* — a little of everything.
2. He took a *scattershot* approach to job hunting.
3. The essay is *scattershot* but full of good lines.

---

### scenic

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsiːnɪk/ &middot; *SEE-nik*
**Русский:** живописный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scenic*, *most scenic*
**Form:** -ic from *scene*
**Contrast:** *The scenic route* is the longer, prettier way — Americans often say it as a joke after getting lost.

Having beautiful natural views.

1. We took the *scenic* route along the coast.
2. There's a *scenic* overlook two miles up the road.
3. The drive through the pass is the *most scenic* part of the trip.

---

### scented

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɛntɪd/ &middot; *SEN-tid*
**Русский:** ароматизированный, душистый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *scent*
**Opposite:** *unscented*
**Contrast:** *Scented* means a smell was added on purpose; *fragrant* means it smells good on its own. American products are labeled *unscented* or *fragrance-free*.

Given a pleasant smell, usually on purpose.

1. She lit a *scented* candle.
2. We bought *scented* soap for the guest bathroom.
3. He hates *scented* laundry detergent.

---

### scheduled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskɛdʒuːld/ &middot; *SKEJ-oold*
**Русский:** запланированный, плановый
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from *schedule*
**Contrast:** Americans say *SKEJ-ool*; the British say *SHED-yool*. A *scheduled* flight is a regular service, not a charter.

Planned to happen at a particular time.

1. The *scheduled* departure is 6:40.
2. *Scheduled* maintenance will close the line tonight.
3. The meeting ran past its *scheduled* end.

---

### schematic

**Pronunciation:** /skiːˈmætɪk/ &middot; *skee-MAT-ik*
**Русский:** схематический, схематичный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more schematic*, *most schematic*
**Contrast:** *A schematic* is also a noun — a wiring or circuit diagram. About writing, *schematic* is a criticism: too simple to feel real.

Shown as a simple diagram, with only the main parts.

1. The manual includes a *schematic* drawing of the circuit.
2. He gave a *schematic* outline of the plan.
3. The characters are *schematic* — more types than people.

---

### scheming

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskiːmɪŋ/ &middot; *SKEE-ming*
**Русский:** коварный, интриганский
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scheming*, *most scheming*
**Form:** -ing from the verb *scheme*
**Opposite:** *straightforward*

Making secret plans to get what you want, usually by tricking people.

1. The show is full of *scheming* lawyers and crooked cops.
2. His *scheming* brother had already sold half the land.
3. She is far *more scheming* than she lets on.

---

### schizophrenic

**Pronunciation:** /ˌskɪtsəˈfrɛnɪk/ &middot; *skit-suh-FREN-ik*
**Русский:** шизофренический; противоречивый
**Type:** classifying; also evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more schizophrenic*, *most schizophrenic*
**Form:** -ic from *schizophrenia*
**Contrast:** The loose sense 'contradictory' offends many people, since it borrows a real illness as a metaphor. In writing, prefer *contradictory* or *inconsistent*.

Schizophrenic has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Having or relating to schizophrenia, a serious mental illness.** (шизофренический)

1. He was treated at the clinic for *schizophrenic* episodes.
2. The book explains what *schizophrenic* symptoms actually look like.
3. Her brother is *schizophrenic* and has been stable for years.

**2. Pulling in two opposite directions at once.** (противоречивый, двойственный)

4. The company's *schizophrenic* marketing sells luxury and thrift in the same ad.
5. Policy on the region has been *schizophrenic* for a decade.

---

### schmaltzy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈʃmɑːltsi/ &middot; *SHMAHLT-see*
**Русский:** слащавый, сентиментальный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *schmaltzier*, *schmaltziest*
**Form:** -y from *schmaltz*, Yiddish for 'chicken fat'

So sentimental that it feels fake or embarrassing.

1. The ending is *schmaltzy*, but I cried anyway.
2. He sang a *schmaltzy* ballad in a rented tuxedo.
3. Their second album is even *schmaltzier* than the first.

---

### scholastic

**Pronunciation:** /skəˈlæstɪk/ &middot; *skuh-LAS-tik*
**Русский:** учебный, школьный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ic from *scholar*

Having to do with schools, studying and grades.

1. Players have to keep up their *scholastic* standing to stay on the team.
2. She won a *scholastic* award for her work in chemistry.
3. The league covers *scholastic* sports across three states.

---

### schoolwide

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskuːlwaɪd/ &middot; *SKOOL-wyd*
**Русский:** общешкольный
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *school* + *-wide*, like *nationwide*

Affecting every part of a school.

1. The principal called a *schoolwide* assembly for Friday morning.
2. They ran a *schoolwide* survey about the new lunch menu.
3. The phone ban is *schoolwide*, not just in the math wing.

---

### scientific

**Pronunciation:** /ˌsaɪənˈtɪfɪk/ &middot; *sy-uhn-TIF-ik*
**Русский:** научный
**Type:** classifying; also quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scientific*, *most scientific*
**Form:** -ic from *science*
**Opposite:** *unscientific*

Scientific has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Having to do with science.** (научный)

1. The journal publishes *scientific* research on sleep.
2. There is no *scientific* evidence for any of this.
3. She left teaching for a *scientific* career at a lab in Boston.

**2. Careful and organized, based on testing rather than guessing.** (методичный, основанный на фактах)

4. He takes a *scientific* approach to picking a fantasy roster.
5. Tasting ten brands in one night is not exactly *scientific*.
6. Their hiring is *more scientific* than it used to be.

---

### scintillating

**Pronunciation:** /ˈsɪntəleɪtɪŋ/ &middot; *SIN-tuh-lay-ting*
**Русский:** блестящий, искромётный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scintillating*, *most scintillating*
**Form:** -ing from the verb *scintillate*, 'to sparkle'
**Opposite:** *dull*
**Contrast:** Often used sarcastically about something boring: *a scintillating lecture on tax forms*.

Brilliantly lively and interesting.

1. She is a *scintillating* speaker who never uses notes.
2. The conversation was hardly *scintillating*.
3. His *scintillating* second half won the game.

---

### scorched

**Pronunciation:** /skɔːrtʃt/ &middot; *SKORCHT*
**Русский:** обожжённый, выжженный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from the verb *scorch*

Burned on the surface, or dried out and browned by heat.

1. Nothing was left but *scorched* fields and black smoke.
2. The shirt had a *scorched* mark where the iron sat too long.
3. By August the lawn was completely *scorched*.

---

### scorching

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskɔːrtʃɪŋ/ &middot; *SKOR-ching*
**Русский:** палящий, знойный; уничтожающий
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scorching*, *most scorching*
**Form:** -ing from the verb *scorch*

Scorching has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Extremely hot.** (палящий, знойный)

1. We walked four blocks in the *scorching* afternoon sun.
2. It was *scorching* by ten in the morning.
3. They played the whole game in *scorching* heat.

**2. Fiercely critical.** (уничтожающий, разгромный)

4. The paper ran a *scorching* review of the restaurant.
5. She sent a *scorching* email to the whole department.

---

### scoreless

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskɔːrləs/ &middot; *SKOR-luhs*
**Русский:** без забитых очков, нулевой
**Type:** classifying
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -less from *score*

With no points scored, in a game or a part of a game.

1. The match ended in a *scoreless* tie.
2. Both teams were *scoreless* through three quarters.
3. The pitcher threw five *scoreless* innings.

---

### scornful

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskɔːrnfəl/ &middot; *SKORN-fuhl*
**Русский:** презрительный
**Type:** emotion
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** more/most — *more scornful*, *most scornful*
**Takes:** *of* + noun or *-ing*
**Form:** -ful from *scorn*
**Opposite:** *admiring*

Showing that you think someone or something deserves no respect.

1. She gave him a *scornful* look and walked out.
2. He is openly *scornful* of anyone who studies for the test.
3. The critics were *more scornful* of the sequel than of the original.

---

### scraggly

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskræɡli/ &middot; *SKRAG-lee*
**Русский:** редкий и неопрятный, клочковатый
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scragglier*, *scraggliest*
**Contrast:** Ends in *-ly* but is an adjective, not an adverb.

Growing in thin, uneven, untidy patches.

1. He showed up with a *scraggly* beard and no jacket.
2. A few *scraggly* pines grew along the ridge.
3. The lawn looked *scragglier* every week we skipped it.

---

### scrambled

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskræmbəld/ &middot; *SKRAM-buhld*
**Русский:** взбитый (о яйцах); перепутанный, зашифрованный
**Type:** participial
**Position:** both — mostly before a noun
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** -ed from the verb *scramble*

Scrambled has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Cooked by beating the eggs together and stirring them in a pan.** (взбитый (о яйцах))

1. I had *scrambled* eggs and toast for breakfast.
2. She likes her eggs *scrambled*, not fried.
3. The diner serves *scrambled* eggs all day.

**2. Mixed up out of order, or coded so that it cannot be read or heard.** (перепутанный; зашифрованный)

4. The letters came through *scrambled* and nobody could read them.
5. The channel is *scrambled* unless you pay for it.

---

### scrappy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskræpi/ &middot; *SKRAP-ee*
**Русский:** задиристый, боевой, упорный
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scrappier*, *scrappiest*
**Contrast:** In American English this is praise — small but tough. British *scrappy* means 'messy, thrown together', which is an insult.

Fighting hard and never giving up, especially against bigger opponents.

1. They are a *scrappy* team that wins games they should lose.
2. He was a *scrappy* kid who never backed down.
3. The *scrappier* of the two candidates took the debate.

---

### scratchy

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskrætʃi/ &middot; *SKRACH-ee*
**Русский:** колючий; хриплый, скрипучий
**Type:** quality
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scratchier*, *scratchiest*
**Form:** -y from *scratch*

Scratchy has 2 distinct senses.

**1. Rough enough against the skin to itch.** (колючий, царапающий)

1. The blanket is warm but *scratchy*.
2. She hated the *scratchy* tag inside the collar.
3. This sweater is *scratchier* than the wool one.

**2. Rough and uneven in sound.** (хриплый, скрипучий)

4. His voice was *scratchy* after two nights of shouting.
5. The recording is *scratchy*, but you can hear the words.

---

### scrawny

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskrɔːni/ &middot; *SKRAW-nee*
**Русский:** тощий, костлявый
**Type:** size
**Position:** both
**Comparison:** -er/-est — *scrawnier*, *scrawniest*
**Opposite:** *burly*
**Contrast:** *Scrawny* is unkind; *slim* and *slender* are compliments.

Unpleasantly thin and weak-looking.

1. He was a *scrawny* teenager who ate constantly.
2. A *scrawny* cat was waiting on the porch.
3. The chicken they served was even *scrawnier* than the last one.

---

### screwball

**Pronunciation:** /ˈskruːbɔːl/ &middot; *SKROO-bawl*
**Русский:** сумасбродный, эксцентричный
**Type:** evaluative
**Position:** attributive only
**Comparison:** not gradable
**Form:** compound — *screw* + *ball*, first a baseball pitch
